17,2026-10-05,"Elianor Williamson is a 36-year-old living in Seattle. Business Insider has verified Williamson's income and employment history with documentation. The following as-told-to essay has been edited for length and clarity.
I went to school for wine. My major was originally education, but while studying abroad for a year in Ireland, I took a class on global food and beverage, and loved it. I switched majors to global wine studies — a little bit of the marketing world and a lot of the wine world.
I was drawn to it because it seemed like a fun, cool, shiny, interesting career path. Salary wasn't really discussed in my two years of studying; looking back now, that was disappointing.
After graduating from Central Washington University in June 2013, I worked in the wine industry for about half a year at a tasting room, pouring wine for people and helping at events.
I was broke and didn't feel I could make any money in that space. I wanted to start a corporate career.
Here's my salary journey since then.
Later in 2013, I found a nonprofit in my area that was hiring for an event coordinator role for wine-related events, like fundraisers where we'd auction off wine for donations.
My offer was around $22,000, my first salary. I was thrilled; before, I'd been paid hourly. I had an office that I went into every day.
I did that for about a year, and I realized there was a sort of ceiling on salary for nonprofit work.
Salary Journeys is Business Insider's first-person series following real people and their real paychecks — how they built their income, what it means for their lives, and what they learned along the way. If you're willing to share how your salary has changed over time, please fill out this Google Form.
A friend of a friend worked at a marketing agency, which I knew nothing about, but he got me an interview.
I was offered a role as a marketing manager in 2015, and they asked what I was looking for from a salary perspective. I'd read all these articles about not being the first one to share a number, so I was like, ""Well, it's going to have to be a lot more than what I'm making now,"" which wasn't helpful, because I hadn't told them what I was making.
We went back and forth, and finally I asked for between $40,000 and $45,000. They came back and offered me $45,000.
Again, I was thrilled with that. I moved out and got my own big girl apartment with a roommate.
I was at that marketing agency for three years, during which I had three different titles, and one promotion in 2016. I was promoted to a program manager, and my salary at that point was around $61,000.
In April 2018, I was like, ""I actually kind of might hate this. I don't know what I want to do with my life."" So, I quit with nothing lined up.
Create a BI Job Scout and get opportunities matched to your goals.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
Everyone in my life thought I was insane, but I just needed time to figure things out.
I was unemployed, job searching, and applying to things. It only took six weeks for me to get an offer for a 6-month contract project manager role at Zillow, project managing content that would be shared at a big event for real estate agents.
I was paid $40 an hour, or about $83,200 annually.
When my contract was ending, I wanted to convert to full-time, so I pushed and worked with my manager to make it happen.
In November 2018, I was converted to a full-time employee at Zillow with the same title — project manager. However, they bumped up my salary to $90,000, and I also got some stock options and other full-time benefits.
Because the equity we got as employees vested over a period of four years, I don't really consider that a huge incentive or part of my income, because I wasn't able to take advantage of that.
A year later, there was a reorganization, and my title changed. I moved to a different team as a campaign manager, and my salary bumped up to $101,000.
This was a huge milestone for me because I was finally making six digits, which I'd been wanting to hit for a while. I was so excited and celebrated with my husband.
I did that for two years.
In 2022, there was another reorg within Zillow, and I got a promotion to senior project manager. My salary was $135,000.
The environment at Zillow had changed under new leadership, so I decided to look for something else.
Later that year, a former colleague got me an interview at a real estate tech startup called OJO.
Once I got the offer and was negotiating my salary, she also encouraged me to ask for more money —so I did.
I was a senior program manager with a salary of $150,000. I also got a $10,000 signing bonus, which was great. I did that for a year.
OJO was acquired by the Royal Bank of Canada in 2023 and rebranded as Houseful.
I made a lateral move to be a senior program and operations manager. Six months later, I was making $155,000, plus a 20% annual cash bonus, which I'd never had before. It was huge.
That year, my total compensation was over $180,000, which was absolutely mind-blowing to me. I was ecstatic.
In March 2025, I was impacted by layoffs. I was unemployed for about five months and collected unemployment. It was a super stressful time.
I started a TikTok account, where I shared my experience of job searching and interviewing hardships, and the mental energy it requires to keep going.
In August 2025, I started as a global brand operations manager at Starbucks.
I was originally offered a salary of $131,500. I was terrified to negotiate; I was desperate for a job and would've 1000% accepted it at that salary. But I knew I'd be disappointed; I know my worth and what my work experience warrants in the market.
After researching the best ways to negotiate, I emailed them back three different options I was comfortable with. I gave a combination of more PTO, a higher salary, a higher bonus, or a bigger signing bonus.
I ended up accepting an offer of $145,000 with a 10% annual bonus and stock. Although I'm technically making $10,000 less in salary than what I was making before, the gap is significantly bigger if you consider my previous full compensation of $180,000. I'd love to get back to that. I think it'll probably take a couple of years.
It's been hard to feel like I took a huge step backward, but I'm grateful to work at Starbucks. I love the brand, and it's a great fit for me right now. It's hybrid, and I love going to the office and seeing people.
There's something in me that ties success to money and my career. I don't know if that comes from a societal narrative or if I've just always been a very driven and determined person. I want to do good work and to feel like I'm contributing.
From a logistical standpoint, more money allows me to live the life that I want to live. I feel like $250,000 to $300,000 in total compensation is what I would be very content with. My husband and I prioritize travel; I want to go on two vacations a year. We love nice things. We want to get a nicer car and have a nicer wardrobe.
My husband owns his own business, so we could invest in more of his videography equipment. We could invest in a second home, maybe a rental property. We could retire early, at 60 or even 55.
It's wild to think that when I was making $45,000, I was living in a two-bedroom apartment and going out every weekend and living the dream. I was contributing to my 401(k), and I had a car. It's a bit of a whiplash to be now making $100,000 more and still be like, ""I want more money.""
But 10 years later, we bought a new house, and have a higher mortgage. Interest rates are insane. And we have a toddler, so we're thinking about daycare costs and a college fund we probably need to start for him. Our priorities have changed.
I'm in my mid-30s, and it's hard not to compare yourself to what other people are doing in this stage of their life; there's such a spectrum. I think no matter what anybody chooses to do, it's fine. Follow your own path.

18,2026-10-05,"You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?
Here’s how AI agents can help companies scale marketing by combining skills, tools and context into an agentic marketing operating model.
Copied to clipboard

		Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.	
There is a unique opportunity in the market to create “agency” within marketing departments with the power of AI agents. I have been in marketing for more than 25 years, and I could not be more excited to scale marketing with AI. Let me introduce you to “Agentic Marketing”
As a company grows, marketing quickly becomes a collection of specialized functions. Product marketing defines positioning and messaging. Demand generation creates pipeline. Creative teams build assets. Growth marketers reach the right accounts. Social and paid media distribute content. Analytics measures performance. Marketing operations connects the systems. In many industries, legal and compliance also need to review what reaches customers.
Large companies may have hundreds of people across these functions. Startups do not have that luxury. And actually, beyond the top 500 companies, many organizations don’t have that marketing investment as well.
AI agents are beginning to change the equation.
I call this shift Agentic Marketing, a new way of executing marketing where AI agents can reason, use specialized skills, access business context and take actions across marketing workflows.
The most important word is agency; generative AI helped marketers create faster. Agentic AI can increasingly help them execute. For entrepreneurs, that could make sophisticated marketing capabilities available much earlier in the life of a company.
Marketing has used automation for decades. A prospect downloads an ebook, and an automated workflow sends an email. A lead reaches a certain score, and the system routes it to sales. A customer abandons a shopping cart, and a follow-up campaign starts automatically.
These systems are valuable, but most traditional marketing automation follows predetermined rules: if this happens, do that. Agentic systems can handle a broader objective.
Imagine telling your marketing system, “Launch a campaign for our new product targeting the top 50 financial services accounts.”
There is no single workflow that can answer that request. The system needs to understand the product, identify the right personas, research target accounts, determine the messaging, create campaign assets, check brand requirements, validate claims, select channels, launch approved content and measure performance.
That requires the system to interpret an objective, determine what needs to happen and take authorized actions toward the outcome.
That is where Agentic Marketing begins.
Consider how a traditional marketing organization works. Product marketing develops positioning and go-to-market strategy. Demand generation turns that strategy into campaigns. Creative teams build the assets. Account-based marketing teams personalize programs for high-value customers. Social and paid media teams distribute the content.
Marketing operations connects the systems, analytics evaluates performance and legal reviews claims before they reach customers. Each function contributes a different capability. Agentic Marketing creates an opportunity to represent some of those repeatable capabilities as specialized AI skills.
A Product Marketing Agent might analyze competitors and develop a messaging brief. A Customer Insights Agent could identify recurring customer pain points. An ABM Agent might research a target account and determine which value propositions are most relevant. A Brand Agent could review content against established standards. A Performance Agent could analyze campaign results and recommend changes. An Orchestrator Agent could coordinate the work across all of them.
For startups, this is a meaningful shift. Instead of asking how many people are required to build every marketing function, founders can start asking which capabilities can be turned into repeatable, agent-powered workflows.
The Agentic Marketing Framework
I believe companies can start building an Agentic Marketing operating model around three components: Skills, Tools and Context.
When these work together, they create agency.
Start with the work your marketing team repeatedly performs.
Maybe product marketing follows a specific process for analyzing competitors. Your content team may use a framework for evaluating whether messaging is focused on customer outcomes. Your social team may have rules for adapting long-form content into short-form posts. Your brand team likely reviews assets against the same standards repeatedly. These processes can become reusable AI skills.
A skill should represent how your company wants a specific job performed. Instead of repeatedly asking an AI model, “Is this good content?” you can create a Content Review Skill that understands what good content means for your organization. The more repeatable the process, the stronger the candidate for a skill.
Skills become far more useful when agents can act. A campaign agent might need access to a CRM. A content agent may need a content management system. An analytics agent needs campaign performance data. A social agent needs access to publishing platforms.
Connecting agents to these systems moves AI beyond answering questions and into executing work. This is also where governance matters. Agents should receive only the permissions required for their role. Some actions can happen automatically. Others should require approval. The goal is controlled autonomy.
Context may be the most important part of the model.
A generic AI model knows very little about your company. It does not inherently know your positioning, customer personas, approved messaging, competitive landscape, pricing strategy, previous campaigns, brand standards or legal requirements. That context is what allows agents to make better decisions.
There is a major difference between asking, “Create a campaign for CFOs,” and giving an agent access to your product messaging, customer research, previous campaign performance, brand guidelines, customer references and target account data before making the same request. Skills define how work gets done. Tools determine what actions can be taken. Context helps the system understand what to do and why.
Skills + Tools + Context = Agency.
Marketing directly shapes how customers experience your company, so autonomy needs guardrails. A poorly designed agentic system can distribute inaccurate information, make inappropriate claims or generate enormous amounts of low-quality content very quickly. Nobody needs more AI slop.
As content becomes cheaper to create, the quality bar should rise. That is why I believe marketing leaders should think about moving from human-in-the-loop toward human-on-the-loop systems.
Humans define the objective, establish the context, create the guardrails and determine which decisions agents can make autonomously. They supervise the system and step in when a decision carries greater brand, financial, legal or customer risk. The opportunity is to increase the speed of the marketing engine while preserving accountability and quality.
Startups have always faced the same constraint: ambition scales faster than resources.
A small company may compete against an organization with hundreds of marketers, yet it still needs strong positioning, compelling creative, personalized campaigns, account research, analytics and consistent execution.
Agentic Marketing can help close part of that capability gap.
The best place to start is small. Identify one repetitive workflow. Turn it into a skill. Connect it to the right tools. Give the agent the business context it needs. Set clear guardrails. Then move to the next workflow.
Over time, those individual capabilities can become an interconnected marketing system.
This is why I believe the real transformation in marketing goes far beyond generating more content. AI is beginning to participate in how marketing work itself gets planned, coordinated and executed.
Generative AI gave entrepreneurs access to intelligence. Agentic AI gives that intelligence agency.
There is a unique opportunity in the market to create “agency” within marketing departments with the power of AI agents. I have been in marketing for more than 25 years, and I could not be more excited to scale marketing with AI. Let me introduce you to “Agentic Marketing”
As a company grows, marketing quickly becomes a collection of specialized functions. Product marketing defines positioning and messaging. Demand generation creates pipeline. Creative teams build assets. Growth marketers reach the right accounts. Social and paid media distribute content. Analytics measures performance. Marketing operations connects the systems. In many industries, legal and compliance also need to review what reaches customers.
Large companies may have hundreds of people across these functions. Startups do not have that luxury. And actually, beyond the top 500 companies, many organizations don’t have that marketing investment as well.

19,2026-10-05,"The county alleges the company collected sensitive data from children’s devices, including where they live and study. A separate investor suit alleges it overstated how constantly its AI models improved.
San Diego County has sued AppLovin, a mobile advertising technology company. The county alleges that AppLovin bypassed parental controls to serve ads for adult dating services, alcohol, vaping and graphic sexual content in games played by children. The complaint was filed with San Diego Superior Court on Monday, Bloomberg reported.
According to the complaint, AppLovin collected sensitive data from children’s devices even when parental controls and ad-tracking opt-outs were enabled. This included “information precise enough to pinpoint where kids live, where they study and whether they are sleeping”.
Apple and Google offer ways to block ad tech companies from tracking children’s digital activity. The complaint says AppLovin bypassed these settings with fingerprinting. It used the technique to track children for ad targeting without parental consent, the complaint alleges.
AppLovin “disregards child safety tools to harvest children’s sensitive personal information” to “feed it into the company’s artificial intelligence advertising engine”, the complaint says.
The suit alleges that AppLovin shows ads depicting sex acts, such as bondage, in apps marked appropriate for kids. One screenshot in the complaint shows an ad for a sexualised AI chatbot app in a physics-based puzzle game. The game was installed on an Android device configured for a six-year-old, with Google’s parental controls enabled. Another screenshot shows ads for cannabis gummies on a device with the same controls.
AppLovin did not immediately respond to Bloomberg’s request for comment.
“AppLovin does not knowingly collect personal information from children or serve Advertisements to children,” its policies for ad buyers say.
The policies, in force since 18 June, prohibit sexually explicit or other adult content, tobacco and nicotine, and illegal drugs. Alcohol ads need AppLovin’s approval. Ads to children, and ads for products generally prohibited for under-18s, are barred. Advertisers are “solely responsible” for determining whether a user qualifies as a child, the policies say.
In 2025, short sellers Fuzzy Panda and Muddy Waters accused AppLovin of harvesting proprietary identifiers from other platforms in an unauthorised manner. They said it used them to track users across websites and apps. Chief executive Adam Foroughi wrote in a March 2025 blog post that the reports were “littered with inaccuracies”. He denied creating “alternative accurate and persistent identifiers, typically called device fingerprints”.
The US Securities and Exchange Commission probed AppLovin’s data collection, Bloomberg reported in October 2025, citing people familiar with the matter. The SEC was responding to a whistleblower complaint and the short-seller reports, they said. It had not accused AppLovin of wrongdoing. Chief financial officer Matt Stumpf said on an earnings call in August that the agency had closed its investigation without recommending enforcement action.
The San Diego case is one of three filed on Monday by the county’s new Consumer Fairness and Public Protection Unit. Local officials set it up in response to what they describe as dwindling federal enforcement of consumer protections. In July, a judge cleared several US states to try their claim that Meta hooked children on Facebook and Instagram.
The unit is also suing Roblox, for inadequate protections to stop adults posing as children and contacting young users. Roblox already faces other lawsuits over child safety. In May, two advocacy groups asked the FTC to investigate it. The third suit targets 3D-printing materials firm Polymaker, for marketing certain materials to people assembling illegal, untraceable guns.
AppLovin also faces a securities class action in the US District Court for the Northern District of California. Stephen Talbot filed it on 16 September. It names the company, Foroughi and Stumpf.
The complaint covers investors who bought or acquired AppLovin securities between 12 February and 5 August 2026. AppLovin told investors it was “constantly improving” its AI models, the complaint says.
“We don’t really see a reason why that’s going to slow down,” the company told investors, according to the complaint.
The suit alleges that the defendants overstated how constantly AppLovin was improving its AI models. It says they overstated the benefits of the “virtuous cycle” and “compounding” value proposition the models offered. It also alleges that the generative AI video creative feature for AppLovin Ads faced significant development delays. These made its release on AppLovin’s timeline unlikely, the suit alleges.
On 13 July, a Bank of America Securities analyst note said that “AppLovin’s eCommerce footprint expanded at a slower pace in June”. Weekly data had “not shown a clear uptick” since AppLovin Ads opened to all e-commerce advertisers on 22 June, the note said. The shares fell $64.13, or 12.65%, from $506.80 to $442.85.
After the market closed on 5 August, AppLovin reported quarterly revenue of $1.92bn, against consensus estimates of $1.94bn. It attributed the miss in part to delays in rolling out the video tool, according to the complaint. The shares fell $82.13 the next day, or 19.66%, from $417.80 to $335.67.
“Our pace of meaningful model improvement was lighter than normal during the quarter,” management said on 5 August, according to the complaint.
Foroughi also said the video tool was “still [a] work in progress”, the complaint says. AppLovin lost more than $44bn of its market capitalisation across the two falls, according to law firm Hagens Berman. The firm has opened an investigation into the claims.
Investors have until 16 November to ask the court to appoint them to lead the case.

20,2026-10-05,"TikTok’s AI shopping assistant answers questions on sizing, shipping and stock, and Buy Direct takes the order in one click. TikTok also opened its ad network to US advertisers.
TikTok is adding an AI shopping assistant and one-click checkout to its app. The two features let users buy directly from brands without leaving TikTok, the company said in an announcement at Advertising Week New York on Monday.
Buy Direct is an in-app checkout. Users can buy from a brand straight from the For You feed with one click, TikTok said. Shopping Assistant is a conversational AI agent that remembers what a user needs. It answers questions about product details, shipping, sizing and availability, and can complete the purchase in the same conversation.
TikTok said it is building the features with commerce platforms and payment providers, including Salesforce, Shopify, Shoplazza and Stripe. It did not give a launch date.
“AI is transforming how advertiser content is created and scaled,” said David Kaufman, TikTok’s global head of monetisation product partnerships.
TikTok also announced Agentic Leads, an AI agent that holds conversations with potential customers to qualify them as sales leads. It will run in TikTok direct messages and in lead forms on advertisers’ own websites.
The TikTok Ad Network is now open to US advertisers. It lets them run campaigns across nearly 400,000 apps outside TikTok, the company said.
TikTok’s advertising tools now also work as a connector in AI platforms including Claude, Kimi, Manus, Perplexity and Replit. The connector uses the Model Context Protocol, an open standard for linking AI agents to other software. The number of advertisers using it to run campaigns has risen by more than 200% since launch, TikTok said, citing its own data for July to September.
Other updates automate search ads, pull AI-generated and creator content into one approval step, and factor costs such as affiliate commissions into TikTok Shop ad campaigns.
The checkout and assistant work across the app, not only inside TikTok Shop, the marketplace TikTok launched in the US in September 2023. Market research firm eMarketer estimates TikTok Shop made about $15.8bn in US sales in 2025, TechCrunch reported. That is roughly 18% of all US social commerce.
Other companies are also building shopping into AI tools. Synchrony wants shoppers’ store cards inside ChatGPT. More than half of US consumers now use AI for shopping, according to NIQ.

21,2026-10-05,"The new visual format is being tested in the US this month, but ads have run in 31 European countries since August, and the Digital Services Act obliges OpenAI to publish a full record of them by January
OpenAI’s mockup of its new visual ad format, with a carousel ad from an invented brand appearing during image generation in ChatGPT.
OpenAI is testing a visual ad format during image generation in ChatGPT, placed by guardrails that assess whether a conversation is emotionally vulnerable or sensitive before an ad appears. Ads already run in 31 European countries, and ChatGPT’s designation as a very large online search engine means it must publish a public ad repository by January.
OpenAI is putting advertisements inside ChatGPT’s image generation. The system decides where they go by judging whether a conversation is emotionally vulnerable. In Europe it will have to publish a record of all of it by January.
The visual format shows products in use or the experiences they allow, is clearly labelled and stays separate from the image being made, OpenAI said. Testing starts later this month in the United States with an initial group of advertisers. Advertising does not influence the answers ChatGPT gives, the company said.
ChatGPT reaches 1.2 billion people a week, by OpenAI’s own count, which it calls the largest AI-native consumer platform in the world. Advertising lets it serve a bigger market than subscriptions alone, the company said.
Europe already has the ads, and has had them for six weeks.
They arrived in 31 European countries in late August, for free and Go users only. Plus, Pro and Enterprise subscribers do not see them.
The regulator arrived in the same week.
The Commission designated ChatGPT a very large online search engine on 31 August, on 159.1 million monthly users in the EU against a threshold of 45 million. ChatGPT Ads passed $1B annualised in under 200 days.
The duties start in January, four months after the notification.
They include a public advertisement repository holding a year of ads, with the advertiser, the runtime, the targeting parameters and the reach. The Commission chose search engine over platform deliberately, to avoid handing OpenAI a liability shield over its own output.
That choice has a side effect on advertising that nobody appears to have intended.
The Digital Services Act bars ads targeted using sensitive personal data, and that rule is written for providers of online platforms. ChatGPT is a search engine.
OpenAI’s placement guardrails assess whether a conversation is emotionally vulnerable, sensitive or otherwise unsuitable before an ad appears. The Commission’s designation named risks to users’ mental well-being among the things OpenAI must now assess.
DoubleVerify and Integral Ad Science will evaluate how the brand safety standards are applied, in a controlled environment and without access to real conversations. OpenAI says the ads are clearly labelled and stay distinct from its answers.

22,2026-10-05,"After five frantic weeks, the Women's Super League takes a two-week break, offering the perfect chance to take stock of a thrilling start to the season. 
Here are five key takeaways from the opening weeks.
Alexia Putellas (right) already has three WSL goals since joining London City from Barcelona
For the first time this decade, the established order of the WSL looks under threat.
London City Lionesses and Tottenham have been bullish about climbing the table, while Liverpool also sought to kick on this season.
After five games, London City look the most convincing, with Sunday's statement 6-1 win at Spurs a dazzling example of the talent and depth manager Eder Maestre has at his disposal.
Two-time Ballon d'Or winner Alexia Putellas headlined some eye-catching summer signings and she scored twice against Tottenham.
But it was the manner in which the whole team dismantled their previously impressive opponents that was so spectacular, with fellow forwards Kadidiatou Diani and Danielle van de Donk also looking a class above.
""This kind of win can be a game-changer,"" Maestre said.
No doubt. It is the kind of display that can lead to talk of a title charge.
For Spurs, a humiliating home loss is a reality check after promising early results.
Liverpool acquitted themselves well in narrow losses away to both Manchester clubs but, with key attacking players such as Vivien Endemann missing against United, they lacked creativity. 
That points to perhaps the biggest thing both Liverpool and Spurs lack in comparison with London City: squad depth.

'It all clicked today' - Van de Donk and Putellas thrilled with 6-1 win against Spurs

Man City fight back to beat struggling Arsenal
Five wins from five with 16 goals scored, defending champions Manchester City have picked up where they left off.
Sunday's comeback victory over Arsenal made it City's best start to a season since opening the 2017-18 campaign with seven straight victories, cementing their status as the team to beat.
In so doing, City also likely ended the Gunners' title aspirations even this early on.
It helps when their best players are performing. Khadija Shaw's contract renewal in the summer was key and the striker has five league goals already. England winger Lauren Hemp is also in fine form, contributing four goals and two assists.
After some quietly shrewd additions in Beth Mead, Carlotta Wamser and Niamh Charles, City look well set once again.
Their closest rivals are Chelsea, who have responded to an opening-day draw against Aston Villa by winning four straight games without conceding a goal.
While boss Sonia Bompastor wants them to be more clinical, their defensive strength is something they have over City.
As for Arsenal, the gap is 10 points to leaders City, and strong pre-season hopes of a first league title since 2018-19 are vanishing, with manager Renee Slegers conceding on Sunday: ""At the moment, we are not in the conversation.""
This video can not be played
Man Utd make it back-to-back wins against Liverpool
Manchester United's summer of upheaval bled into the start of the season, with Eva Olid's arrival from Hearts beginning with convincing defeats by London City and Chelsea.
The message from the club was that this would be a season of transition but recent performances suggest they are getting to grips with her style of play.
Saturday's 1-0 win over Liverpool was United's fourth straight victory in all competitions, with 18 goals scored in the process.
""I am so happy for the players - they deserve it,"" Olid said. ""We have started to click in a lot of things and they fight for each other.
""But there is still a lot to improve. We created chances, but our last pass and our finishing needs to be better.""
Summer signing Andrea Medina has been a huge positive on the left wing, with her contribution to the team more pronounced than a single goal suggests.
A trip to bottom side Charlton after the break is a presentable chance for more points before a stern test against Manchester City the following weekend.

Charlton thrashed by Brighton despite taking lead
This season is the WSL's biggest ever, literally, with the league expanding from 12 to 14 teams and three coming up from WSL 2.
But does bigger mean stronger?
Crystal Palace look the most comfortable of the promoted sides, boasting six points from five games and they moved to eighth in the WSL table by drawing at Aston Villa on Sunday.
But things are more difficult for the other promoted sides, Birmingham and Charlton, who prop up the table.
Despite some promising patches, WSL 2 champions Birmingham have just two points and one of those came against Crystal Palace.
At least France forward Oceane Hurtre looks suited to this level, sitting as joint top scorer in the WSL with five goals from five games.
Hurtre scored against Everton in defeat on Sunday but defender Neve Herron summed things up by saying Blues were ""second to everything"" after the game.
Meanwhile, it looks bleak for Charlton, bottom of the table with zero points, 15 goals against and only two goals scored.
After their 5-1 loss to Brighton on Sunday, goalkeeper and captain Sophie Whitehouse said: ""It just wasn't good enough.
""It's a tough league. The jump up is so big. You can't make any mistakes.""
Finally, it is too soon to draw conclusions but attendances at some of the league's biggest matches have been down on previous seasons.
For instance, Chelsea hosted Arsenal on 27 September in a fervent atmosphere, but the game had 36% fewer in Stamford Bridge than in January this year.
Similarly, Liverpool against Everton at Anfield was 19% down on last season, albeit the Merseyside derby was the first game of the 2025-26 season.
Three Saturdays ago, Arsenal kicked off against Manchester United at 17:30 in front of 29,853 fans. Last season, it was a lunchtime fixture that welcomed 37,627.
For balance, it ought to be highlighted that the Manchester City v Arsenal game on Sunday was promoted from the Joie Stadium to Etihad Stadium, and definitely had more than the 4,300 who attended the game last season.
Official figures have yet to be released for it. 
Nevertheless, while this is a small sample size, paying customers is a potential trend to monitor as the WSL wrestles with the continued challenge of the schedule.

23,2026-10-05,"India has one of the world's worst performing major equity markets in 2026
India's economy is growing at an enviable rate of over 7% despite global energy shocks, rising interest rates, tariff uncertainties and weather-related disruptions.
But the world's fastest growing major economy also has one of the worst performing major equity markets in 2026. The correction in Indian stocks has, in fact, only intensified in recent weeks.
The benchmark Sensex and Nifty indices, which represent the country's largest companies, have inched up slightly since Monday after posting losses for eight straight weeks - the longest losing streak in 25 years, according to Reuters.
Indian mom-and-pop investors who put their money into the Nifty have seen their wealth erode by about 15% this year. In comparison, they would have made 62% returns on Korea's Kospi index since January or 170% in the last two years.
On aggregate, the money foreign investors have put into Indian markets in the past decade - after subtracting what they sold or withdrew - is nearing zero. In the past two years alone, foreign institutional investors have withdrawn a staggering $40bn, according to data from Bernstein Research.
It is the large pool of domestic institutional and retail money, flowing into instruments like mutual funds, that have helped the markets avoid a sharper fall.
Domestic assets under management of mutual funds have grown from about $125bn in 2016 to some $900bn this year, with the number of Indians parking money in stocks and mutual funds more than tripling to 150 million individuals.
This makes the recent fall in the markets more worrying - since households, already struggling from a weak job market, high inflation and faltering consumption, are now seeing their equity savings take a beating too.
So, what's gone wrong? 
Here are five reasons India's booming economy isn't lifting its stock market.
India imports over 90% of its crude requirements
Crude oil prices have hovered between $90 and $100 a barrel as the disruption to shipping through the Strait of Hormuz enters its eighth month, far longer than analysts had expected. Moreover, there's little visibility on when normalcy will return.
""This single variable tends to influence the markets quite negatively,"" Hari Shyamsunder, a fund manager with Franklin Templeton Asset Management India, told the BBC. ""Markets can absorb crude between $70 and $90, but when prices move above $100 a barrel, it starts putting stress on macro-economic variables such as inflation and also company earnings and margins."" 
India imports over 90% of its requirements - nearly half of its crude oil imports, along with a large share of its liquified petroleum gas (LPG) and liquified natural gas (LNG) shipments, come through the Strait of Hormuz.
While Delhi has diversified its energy sources, tapping Russian oil markets, US President Donald Trump recently threatened up to 100% tariffs on countries doing trade with Moscow, further complicating matters.
With oil prices rising, inflation is going up and, as a result, interest rates are on the rise globally. The effective yield on US government bonds is above 5%, or at near 25-year highs. 
These are often considered risk-free investments. As a result, foreign money tends to leave riskier emerging market assets such as Indian equities and chase safer investments like US bonds when interest rates rise.
For foreign investors, the problem of muted returns has been compounded by a weaker rupee. In currency-adjusted dollar terms, they have suffered because of the fall in the Indian currency.
The Nifty has delivered just 6% annualised dollar returns over the past decade - hardly attractive, especially in comparison with several other competing markets.
Indian stocks are still expensive relative to their earnings compared to other emerging markets 
Stock valuations have been another major concern.
The market correction over the last two years has reduced the premium that Indian stocks held over their emerging market competitors.
""Stocks are cheaper than they have been on average for the last ten years,"" says Shyamsunder, adding that the premium that Indian shares commanded over other emerging economies has shrunk substantially in the past few years.
However, they are still expensive relative to their earnings, especially since companies in countries such as South Korea and Taiwan have benefited from a huge boom in artificial intelligence (AI) which has pushed up their profits. 
In India, AI, or the new economy broadly, is a major missing piece of its growth story.
""Many of India's large caps [big companies] represent a bygone economic era,"" Bernstein Research said in a note recently. ""Most are not investing in the future, but consolidating their past, often expecting policy to continue shielding them from global competition.""
India seeks AI breakthrough - but is it falling behind?
And its smaller companies are yet to reach a scale that will attract foreign institutional funds.
While Indian companies are investing in data centres and chip fabrication, the country has not produced a global giant like OpenAI, Anthropic or even China's DeepSeek - this is where the largest share of the profits in the AI value chain lie.
Foreign investor interest will only return to India meaningfully if it can build globally competitive industries in these emerging areas, according to Bernstein.
""We are seeing early signs of this in areas such as space, defence, semiconductors, and deep-tech innovation, but most remain too small to materially influence capital allocation decisions over much of this decade."" 
Domestic savings into mutual funds haven't reduced despite the market correction
So what's next? 
There are many immediate factors that will influence how Indian markets behave going forward. 
""Easing geopolitical tensions and relatively attractive valuations could support a revival in FPI [foreign portfolio investor] inflows,"" according to CareEdge, a brokerage, though trade tensions and higher energy prices remain a âchallenge for corporate performance going aheadâ.
For now, while foreign investors may have given up, Indian savers, even though increasingly more anxious, haven't slowed their monthly investment flows , externalinto mutual funds. 
The real test will be whether this resilience survives a deeper correction in the future.

24,2026-10-05,"From Patagonia vests to Rolex Submariners, the finance world is full of status symbols and fashion flexes. Wealthy Swiss investors have one of the weirdest: a blue briefcase filled with roughly 10 pounds of chocolate.
Lindt shareholders who vote at the Swiss chocolatier's annual meeting in Zurich every April receive a ""Schokoladekoffer"" or ""chocolate suitcase.""
The briefcase is free, but a Lindt voting share costs around 78,000 Swiss francs, or more than $94,000. The shares have traded as high as 132,000 Swiss francs — north of $150,000 — within the past year.
""Nobody rationally spends around 100,000 Swiss francs on a share because they want 3kg of free chocolate,"" Thierry Borgeat, the cofounder and chief investor of Swiss asset manager arvy, told Business Insider by email on Monday. ""But that is exactly what makes it fun.""
""It's a flex — but a very Swiss kind of flex,"" Borgeat continued. ""You don't buy a Ferrari or a flashy watch to show people you have money. You own one registered share in a 180-year-old Swiss chocolate company, attend the annual meeting, and then quietly walk through Zurich carrying three kilograms of chocolate in a blue suitcase.""
Borgeat recalled that when he worked in wealth management, clients and private bankers would bring their sweet hauls to the office after the annual meeting and share their bars and truffles.
Doing so ""had the convenient side effect of letting everyone know that you owned a full Lindt registered share,"" he said.
Borgeat noted the ""quirky tradition has become so exclusive"" because Lindt has been an ""exceptional long-term investment,"" resulting in its shares becoming ""extraordinarily valuable over time.""
Lindt stock climbed from around 3,600 Swiss francs in April 1995 to 132,000 at its peak last October, a 36-fold increase. But it has slumped in recent months as record temperatures have sapped sales, and historically high cocoa prices have inflated the chocolatier's costs and crimped its profits.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
As many wonder...
                  
                  Every year, the annual shareholders’ meeting takes place; you go there, meet up with people and friends, and leave the hall with a blue suitcase. Hundreds of them...
                  
                  That’s where you’re seen, and that’s the Swiss way of showing off.
                  
                  Strange, I know, but that’s just how it is.
                  
                  It is a flex to own a Lindt share.
                  
                  The Swiss way...
""We consider this a thank you for their efforts,"" a Lindt & Sprüngli spokesperson told Business Insider about the company's yearly gift to voting shareholders.
""The box contains a variety of Lindt products such as Excellence bars and Lindor truffles, but we don't reveal what's exactly inside because it's meant to be a surprise for our shareholders,"" the spokesperson added.
Lawrence Cunningham, the director of the Weinberg Center for Corporate Governance at the University of Delaware, said the gift boxes were ""good marketing and a thoughtful gesture.""
""It thanks shareholders for showing up and puts the company's product in their hands,"" he said, adding that it also fosters goodwill.
The buzzy phenomenon is reminiscent of Berkshire Hathaway's annual meeting. Every May, thousands of shareholders from across the world fly to Warren Buffett's hometown of Omaha, not only to see the legendary investor in person, but also to load up on discounted wares from Berkshire's many businesses, including boxed-chocolate maker See's Candies.
Borgeat said he can ""definitely see the similarities"" between Berkshire and Lindt as ""companies that become more than just investments — they create a sense of identity and community among their shareholders.""
""Berkshire has Omaha and See's Candies,"" he continued. ""Lindt has Zurich and the blue chocolate suitcase.""

Apple released macOS 27.2 beta 3 to developers today, and it includes new references to upcoming support for touchscreen interactivity. As discovered by Aaron Perris for MacRumors, there are “gesture recognition tools” for several stock Apple apps:
macOS 27.2 beta 3 also adds a new long-press animation for the Dock, which could also be a hint at the forthcoming touchscreen MacBook Pro:
macOS 27.2 beta 3 introduces a new long-press animation for Dock icons, hinting at the upcoming touchscreen MacBook Pro pic.twitter.com/4zfZOxy8MO
Apple has gradually made a number of changes to macOS 27 in preparation for touchscreen support, including pull-to-refresh support for the first time. macOS 27 also added touch input to Sidecar, a feature that lets you use your iPad as a secondary display for your Mac.
Bloomberg has also reported on Apple’s work to optimize macOS for the impending touchscreen MacBook Pro. For instance, tapping a button or control will prompt macOS to dynamically display relevant touch options. Similarly, tapping the menu bar will automatically enlarge controls for easier touch navigation.
In addition to a touchscreen, the new MacBook Pro will reportedly have an OLED display and be “significantly lighter” than the current models.
Apple is expected to introduce the new MacBook Pro (or MacBook Ultra?) as soon as this month, though Bloomberg has cautioned it could slip to November.

29,2026-10-05,"Transport and Environment counts 16 electric models under EUR 25,000 on sale by the end of this year, twice last year's number, and models the European Parliament rapporteur's version of the 2035 target as cutting sales of those cars by about three quarters by 2030
Battery-electric sales in the EU reached 1.64 million in the first eight months of 2026, up 45%, with 16 models under EUR 25,000 due on sale by the end of the year. Transport and Environment says the version of the 2035 target proposed by Parliament’s rapporteur would cut sales of those cheap models by nearly three quarters by 2030.
Cheap electric cars are selling in Europe because the rules required them. Battery-electric sales in the EU reached 1.64 million in the first eight months of the year, up 45%. The version of the 2035 target now in front of Parliament would cut sales of the cheapest models by about three quarters by 2030.
That last figure is Transport and Environment’s own modelling, in a report published today. The advocacy group counts 16 models under EUR 25,000 on sale by the end of 2026, twice last year’s number. Sales of those cars are on course to reach seven times their 2024 level.
More than 150 mass-market electric models are now available in the EU. About 60 arrive this year, against an average of 15 a year between 2021 and 2025.
The sales numbers themselves are not new.
We reported the 1,641,333 registrations and the 21.7% share in September, when battery-electric drew level with petrol for the year. Transport and Environment puts the eight-month share at 22%, and August alone at 28%.
France reached 38% in August and Germany 32%.
The argument is about what comes next. Massimiliano Salini, the Forza Italia member who is Parliament’s rapporteur, wants the 2035 target set at a 90% cut rather than an end to combustion, which is also the Commission’s position. His report would treat cars running only on renewable fuels as equivalent to electric ones.
It would also stretch the compliance period from three years to five.
Transport and Environment models the Salini version at 22% battery-electric in 2030, unchanged from today, and 39% in 2035, against 47% and 85% under the Commission’s. On those numbers European carmakers sell about 5.7 million battery cars in 2035 and Chinese carmakers sell 11.9 million.
European manufacturers supply nearly 60% of the models on sale today, having added 16 in the first half. Chinese brands hold 21% and added 11.
The compliance record so far does not support the case for relief. Carmakers have closed three quarters of the gap to the 2025 to 2027 targets halfway through the period, and the group expects all of them to comply. BMW, Stellantis, Kia and the Mercedes-Volvo pool are already there.
Volkswagen has about 7 grams of CO2 per kilometre left to find.
Britain, outside all of this, took 29.8% in August under the mandate it kept, against 28% across the EU.

30,2026-10-05,"Its process makes new cement in the furnaces that already recycle steel, with no kiln and no quarried limestone. Clean Growth Fund and AP Ventures co-led the Series A round.
From left: Stephen Price (Clean Growth Fund), George Irven (AP Ventures), Bill Yost (Reclinker), Gareth Mayhead (Development Bank of Wales), Poppy Brewer (Reclinker), Ben Woodhouse (Clean Growth Fund), Pippa Horton (Reclinker), Alex Garavel (AP Ventures) and Cyrille Dunant (Reclinker)
Reclinker, a startup that turns demolition waste back into cement, has raised £10m in a Series A round. It will use the money to move from trials to full commercial production in Cardiff. The company said on Monday that its process cuts cement’s emissions in half, at the same cost as conventional cement.
Clean Growth Fund and AP Ventures co-led the round. The Development Bank of Wales and Kibo Invest joined, along with existing investors Zero Carbon Capital and Cambridge Enterprise. Chief executive Bill Yost said the round was oversubscribed.
“This oversubscribed round sends a strong signal that UK investors are serious about decarbonisation,” Yost said.
Cement causes about 7% of global CO₂ emissions, roughly three times as much as aviation, according to Reclinker. Most of it comes from making clinker, cement’s key ingredient. Kilns heat limestone, which releases CO₂ from the rock and from the fossil fuels that fire the kilns.
Researchers at the University of Cambridge developed Reclinker’s process and described it in a 2024 paper in Nature. It recovers old cement paste from demolished buildings. That paste then goes into the electric arc furnaces that already recycle scrap steel, where it turns back into clinker.
The furnace makes recycled steel and new cement at the same time. The process needs no kiln, no quarried limestone and no fossil-fuel heat. Reclinker expects a 50% cut in emissions at first, and more as the technology scales. TNW has not independently verified these figures.
Other heavy industries are also recycling their own waste into new material. Porsche has built a battery cell with a recycled cathode, made from its own old packs.
Reclinker has made thousands of tonnes of its product at 7 Steel UK’s plant in Cardiff. It has also secured its first commercial sales. It targets a gap in supply. Concrete makers have long used blast-furnace slag for low-carbon cement blends. That slag is running out as steelmakers move away from blast furnaces.
“By investing alongside specialist climate technology investors, we’re helping Reclinker establish and grow its presence here,” said Gareth Mayhead of the Development Bank of Wales.
Through the Innovate UK Advance Market Commitment, large concrete users have said they plan to use more low-carbon materials. They aim to do so over the next five years. Beyond Cardiff, the money will help Reclinker take its process to other electric arc furnace sites in Europe and the US.
Construction technology has drawn large rounds this autumn. SoftBank invested $225m in autonomous construction firm ASI in September, a month after Gravis Robotics raised a $200m Series A led by SoftBank.
“Cement is one of the most challenging sectors to decarbonise, with emissions deeply embedded in both process chemistry and energy use,” said Stephen Price, investment partner at Clean Growth Fund.

31,2026-10-05,"Its own tests show editing the text weakens detection. Text watermarking stays opt-in for API customers worldwide, and the detector is limited to approved researchers.
OpenAI will add an invisible watermark to text from ChatGPT and Codex in the EU over the coming weeks. The change covers eligible users on all plans, but only in the EU, the company said in a blog post on Monday. OpenAI said it is acting in response to the EU AI Act, which requires AI-generated text to be identifiable by machines.
OpenAI is not making text watermarking a global default at launch. The regional approach gives it room to learn from real-world use and feedback, it said. API customers anywhere can opt in for select models from today. Watermarking stays off by default in the API. OpenAI said it is also working with cloud partners to offer it on its models through their services.
The system, called textGrain, adds a hidden statistical signal to the model’s word choices. A detector then looks for that signal. OpenAI said textGrain matched or beat other approaches it tested, including Google’s SynthID for text. It published a technical report written with researchers from the University of Pennsylvania and Yale.
Benchmark scores for its Astra model showed no meaningful difference with the watermark on, OpenAI said. It also plans to release the technology as open source.
OpenAI also set out the limits. At a target false positive rate of 1%, its detector found the watermark in about 80% of 200-token passages on topics such as psychology. For 400-token passages, the rate was about 95%. Replacing 10% of words with synonyms cut detection from about 92% to 66%. Replacing a quarter of the words cut it to 17%. Detection was substantially lower for maths, where word choice is less flexible, the company said.
The watermark does not identify the user, measure human contribution, establish ownership or verify accuracy, OpenAI said. A missing watermark does not prove a human wrote the text either. The text could be too short, edited or translated, or come from another company’s tools.
“These limitations contribute to our decision to provide initial detector access only to approved researchers and expert organizations, who can help us evaluate reliability and responsible uses,” OpenAI wrote.
Applications open today, and access will be granted case by case, in line with the EU’s Code of Practice, OpenAI said. The detector reports whether it finds an OpenAI watermark without identifying the user or revealing prompts. OpenAI said it will widen access once results can be interpreted responsibly. Its tools for checking images and audio remain public.
Article 50 of the AI Act requires generative AI providers to make their text output machine-readable. Providers already on the market have until 2 December to comply, Engadget reported.
Anthropic began watermarking Claude’s text worldwide in August, with some exemptions. Tools to strip AI watermarks have since appeared. Google DeepMind has also watermarked AI-designed proteins with SynthID.

Asking the right questions and listening to the answers can expose blind spots and turn your team’s input into shared ownership.
Copied to clipboard

		Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.	
Early in building my venture fund, I made a mistake many founders make: I worked alone and rushed decisions.
Even after I built a team, I would walk into a meeting, feel like I understood the opportunity and start connecting the dots out loud. I thought I was being decisive. In reality, I had blinders on. I was seeing what I expected to see and missing what I should have been asking. The turning point came when I forced myself to do something different. Instead of talking, I paused and asked a few simple questions. Then I went around the room and got input from the team. What I heard changed the direction of the decision.
My team raised issues I had completely missed. Risks, gaps and constraints that were obvious to them were invisible to me in the moment. In a few cases, they talked me out of decisions I was ready to make. In others, they strengthened the case in ways I hadn’t considered. Either way, the outcome was better because I stepped back and listened.
That shift made something clear: Asking questions isn’t a leadership weakness. It’s a leadership advantage. It makes you sharper, makes your team better and protects your business from blind spots you can’t see on your own. The best entrepreneurs understand this. They don’t try to have all the answers. They learn to ask better questions and actually listen to what comes back. Here are the four types of questions I rely on most.
Every decision benefits from clarity at the starting line. Foundation questions cut through the noise and force focus.
Ask: What problem are we solving? What are you excited about?
I often start conversations with founders this way. It sounds basic, but it quickly reveals whether an idea is grounded or drifting. When founders can clearly explain the problem and why it matters, the rest of the discussion sharpens. When they can’t, there’s work to do before anything else moves forward.
In one conversation with a group of student founders who had just won a design competition, the room was full of excitement. They were energized and eager to talk. Starting with these simple questions brought the conversation back to first principles and gave it structure where there had only been momentum.
Vision matters, but execution lives in the details. Simple, practical questions bring those details into focus.
Ask: Where are you in the build? Do you have a minimum viable product? What does your market actually look like? Have you talked to any potential customers yet?
With those same student founders, a few targeted follow-ups revealed big gaps that wouldn’t have surfaced otherwise. They hadn’t built a minimum viable product, let alone talked to prospective customers. Without those questions, it would have been easy to move forward on enthusiasm alone. With them, the next steps became clear and grounded in reality.
I’ve seen this play out again and again. Teams move faster when constraints are visible early. Practical questions reduce rework by exposing what needs attention before resources are committed.
In my experience, blind spots usually come from a limited perspective. You can widen that perspective by tapping into your team.
Ask: What are we missing? What else should we be thinking about?
This has become one of the most valuable questions I ask. It creates space for ideas that would otherwise go unspoken.
In one team meeting, I closed with a simple version of it. A team member pointed out that our investors would soon expect a performance dashboard with key metrics, something I hadn’t considered. It was the right call at the right time. That moment turned into action. Our analysts and interns built the dashboard, which strengthened how we communicated progress and added value for our investors. The idea didn’t come from the person leading the meeting. It came from making room for others to think out loud.
Great decisions require more than input. They require commitment.
Ask: Where are you on this decision: yes, no or hold? Why?
When we began making investment decisions as a team, I made a point of hearing from everyone, from experienced professionals to student interns. I went person by person and asked where they stood and why.
The impact was immediate. Sometimes the team talked me out of deals I initially favored. Other times, strong alignment gave us the confidence to move forward. Those early investments turned out well, but the process built something more important than any single outcome: shared ownership. People support what they help shape, and alignment questions make that possible. Over time, this became part of our culture. Everyone knew they would be asked, so everyone came prepared, which improved both our decisions and our execution.
You don’t need a formal system to use this approach. You need consistency.
In meetings, one-on-ones and major decisions, build the habit of asking instead of telling. Start with these four questions:
Used consistently, these questions reshape how teams think. They move conversations from assumptions to clarity and replace resistance with ownership.
Entrepreneurial leadership is often framed around decisiveness and speed. Those matter. But leaders who consistently outperform understand something deeper.
They know when to pause, and that a well-timed question can do more than a well-crafted answer. By slowing down at the right moment, they give their teams the chance to move faster where it counts.
Early in building my venture fund, I made a mistake many founders make: I worked alone and rushed decisions.
Even after I built a team, I would walk into a meeting, feel like I understood the opportunity and start connecting the dots out loud. I thought I was being decisive. In reality, I had blinders on. I was seeing what I expected to see and missing what I should have been asking. The turning point came when I forced myself to do something different. Instead of talking, I paused and asked a few simple questions. Then I went around the room and got input from the team. What I heard changed the direction of the decision.
My team raised issues I had completely missed. Risks, gaps and constraints that were obvious to them were invisible to me in the moment. In a few cases, they talked me out of decisions I was ready to make. In others, they strengthened the case in ways I hadn’t considered. Either way, the outcome was better because I stepped back and listened.

by Andrew Limbong, Society & Culture Reporter
In a town not far from Barbara Kingsolver's house in southwestern Virginia is a historic marker noting Marion, Va., as ""the birthplace of modern Mountain Dew."" I came across it on the drive down I-81 to interview the Pulitzer-Prize winning author. It took me by surprise — I didn't expect such a momentous event here, in Appalachia. After all, isn't this the middle of nowhere?
There's some quibbling — the drink's roots trace back to Knoxville, Tennessee, which is why the word ""modern"" is in the designation. But it's here, in Marion, where the Dew truly realized both its potential and its propensity for change.
When I first encountered Kingsolver's work (The Poisonwood Bible, in high school AP English class), I was more partial to Mountain Dew Code Red — the version most associated with hardcore gamers. That didn't end up being my life, though.
Contested memories, an overlooked and condescended-to town, expectations of the future that go awry, how things change (but do they, really?) — we get into all of that in my interview with Kingsolver about her new novel, Partita. It's about a young classical pianist from Appalachia who has the chops to really make it, until her life gets upended. Kingsolver grew up loving classical music, and even went to college on a music scholarship. But that didn't end up being her life, either.

YuChiang Cheng saw a different path forward — and it’s paying off big time.
Copied to clipboard
In 2022, when YuChiang Cheng invested in Hero Bread on behalf of 444 Capital and stepped in as the company’s interim chief marketing officer, the low-carb, zero-sugar brand was at a critical crossroads. 
Cole Glass had founded the company in 2019 to address his severe food allergies related to pollen. By October 2021, a partnership with Subway seemed like an exciting path forward. The idea was to get the macro-friendly bread products into as many hands as possible so people could taste it for themselves. 
However, around the time Cheng joined the company, it was clear the unit economics didn’t make sense. The Subway deal wasn’t profitable; Hero Bread was running out of money. 
“ We were just too small at the time and couldn’t service it, didn’t have the resources and all the infrastructure put in place to effectively deliver that,” Cheng says. “So we backed out of it.”
Cheng pivoted to a direct-to-consumer (DTC) strategy, allowing the brand to receive feedback from consumers more quickly and drive word-of-mouth sales, he says. 
Cheng assumed the role of CEO in November 2023 and has continued to grow the company.
In a funding round announced in June 2024, Hero Bread raised $21 million in under 90 days. The round, co-led by Cleveland Avenue, DNS Capital and Composite Ventures, brought the company’s total funding to date to more than $68.5 million. 
Hero Bread brought in $54 million in annual revenue last year. Additionally, the company more than doubled its retail presence in two years, expanding from about 4,000 to 10,000 doors, which includes a recent Target launch. 
But Hero Bread’s successful push into retail — a feat many CPG brands that launch DTC hope to achieve — doesn’t mean the company plans to put the brakes on its DTC strategy anytime soon.
Entrepreneur sat down with Cheng to learn more about what he calls the “third chapter” of Hero Bread’s DTC playbook, and how it’s continuing to unlock growth for the company. 
After walking away from the Subway partnership and prioritizing DTC sales, Hero Bread focused on five SKUs: white bread, seeded bread, burger buns, hot dog buns and flour tortillas. 
The line-up also made sense as the company entered retailers: The goal was to offer everyday staples that would drive repeat purchases on a weekly basis. 
Initially, though, Cheng and the team weren’t quite sure which retailers would be the right fit. 
“It’s a very mainstream taste profile,” Cheng explains. “So there was a lot of debate, if this is going to be more of a Publix, Target type product, or is this going to be more of a natural Whole Foods, Sprouts, type of product.”
So Hero Bread opted to experiment with different types of retailers. The company launched in Market District, Publix and Sprouts and planned to lean into whichever channel performed best. But six months later, the company’s products had an impressive track record at all three. 
“We were very, very fortunate in that,” Cheng says. “So instead of picking [retail] channels, we decided to pick retailers that were more enthusiastic regardless of what channel they were in.” 
It’s a different tactic than many CPG brands use; they tend to zero in on one retail channel and go regional, the CEO notes. 
Of course, CPG brands that expand into retail have the advantage of economies of scale. 
“You can move tons of pallets on a truck much more affordably than you can send individual packages through ecommerce,” Cheng says. 
As a result, retail typically gives consumers a more affordable way to buy. DTC, on the other hand, serves as a more concierge path to purchase not only Hero Bread’s staple products, but also other offerings that might not be able to hit retail shelves as seamlessly. 
Hero Bread’s Crafted Collection, which includes items like croissants, lemon poppy seed scones, shortbread biscuits (hand-baked in Sonoma with brown butter made by a Michelin chef), lends itself well to the model. The company’s recent collaboration on pretzel bites with Auntie Anne’s does too. 
“ [These are] small-batch, largely handmade products that are exclusive to hero.co, where it just wouldn’t make economic sense for us to hand-roll 300,000 to 400,000 croissants,” Cheng explains. 
What’s more, DTC can act as an invaluable testing ground for products with the potential to sell effectively in a brick-and-mortar setting. 
That’s exactly what the company did with its bagels, unveiling a limited-edition launch online in December 2024. 
“ We weren’t sure if people would want to buy bagels from us,” Cheng says, “so we initially made them in smaller batches, sold them at hero.co, and they quickly became one of our best-selling items.” 
Hero Bread’s bagels landed on retail shelves within six months. 
Cheng doesn’t believe a successful entrepreneur or business leader is defined by how many times they win. 
“It’s how many times you can try again, regardless of how many times you’ve fallen down or how much blood you get on your face,” the CEO says. “There is a level of courage that is really necessary.” 
It’s a tendency toward resilience that’s helped Cheng lead Hero Bread through some crucial years that required an enthusiastic willingness to adapt to every challenge — and the CEO looks forward to what comes next. 
“We’re always trying to meet the consumer where they are,” Cheng says. “There’s a specific Hero fan, and then there’s the overarching bread fan. We believe that the more people who try us, the more people who will believe [in us].”
In 2022, when YuChiang Cheng invested in Hero Bread on behalf of 444 Capital and stepped in as the company’s interim chief marketing officer, the low-carb, zero-sugar brand was at a critical crossroads. 
Cole Glass had founded the company in 2019 to address his severe food allergies related to pollen. By October 2021, a partnership with Subway seemed like an exciting path forward. The idea was to get the macro-friendly bread products into as many hands as possible so people could taste it for themselves. 
However, around the time Cheng joined the company, it was clear the unit economics didn’t make sense. The Subway deal wasn’t profitable; Hero Bread was running out of money.

36,2026-10-05,"Nvidia, Tesla, Apple and Microsoft are among dozens of U.S. stocks that could soon be available to trade 24 hours a day, seven days a week on a new blockchain-based market from crypto exchange OKX and the owner of the New York Stock Exchange.
A regulatory filing from OKXICE, a joint venture between OKX and NYSE owner Intercontinental Exchange, listed more than 60 securities it plans to offer in tokenized form and explained how the new market would work.
The list includes Nvidia, Tesla, Apple, Microsoft, Amazon and Alphabet, as well as crypto-linked companies Coinbase, Circle, Robinhood, Strategy and Securitize. JPMorgan, Goldman Sachs, Walmart, Netflix, Reddit and Boeing are also among the names.
But inclusion in the filing doesn’t guarantee that every stock will trade on the platform, as companies can object to having their shares included during a 30-day window. Cerebras, for example, objected to the inclusion of its stock, according to the filing.
“No symbol is a given,” TD Securities analysts wrote in a report on Monday.
For investors, the easiest way to understand tokenization is to think of a familiar stock represented by a digital token that can be bought and sold on a blockchain.
Take Nvidia. Instead of buying the stock through a traditional brokerage account and having the trade processed through the legacy stock-market system, an investor on OKXICE would buy a digital token representing an entitlement to an Nvidia share.
The filing said the underlying stock would be held by a registered broker-dealer on a one-for-one basis, meaning one Nvidia token would be backed by one Nvidia share. Token holders would also be entitled to the economic and shareholder rights that come with the stock, including dividends and voting rights.
Rather than paying in dollars, investors on the new platform would buy and sell tokenized stocks using digital dollars known as stablecoins. OKXICE plans to support USDC, USDT and USDG, three cryptocurrencies designed to maintain a $1 value.
There also won't be the traditional system of matching a buyer's order with a seller's order. Instead, the stock tokens and digital dollars will sit in blockchain-based pools that investors can trade against. Buying Nvidia tokens takes them out of the pool and adds digital dollars, pushing the price higher. Selling does the reverse.
The pools, known in crypto as automated market makers, or AMMs, use rules to determine the price at which investors can buy and sell. More sophisticated versions can also allow professional trading firms to actively adjust prices and the amount of stock available, potentially making the market behave more like a conventional exchange.
TD Securities said these automated market makers, or AMMs, could take several forms. A conventional version, for example, would set prices using a mathematical formula based on the assets in a pool. But the bank thinks that more actively managed designs could matter more for stock trading.
“We see Prop AMMs and managed Multi-Pool Venues as much more consequential than conventional AMM models,” the analysts said, arguing that liquidity providers can actively adjust prices and inventory and are “less likely to be picked off by stale prices.”
Under that model, a market maker would set prices using market data and its own inventory, while another could combine several liquidity pools into a venue that resembles a traditional stock exchange.
That system is similar to the technology already used by decentralized crypto exchanges, but OKXICE plans to apply it to stocks. The trades would take place on XLayer, a blockchain developed by OKX, and the pools will use decentralized exchange Uniswap’s plumbing.
There is another major difference: trading doesn't stop when Wall Street closes.
OKXICE plans to operate around the clock, including nights and weekends.
That means an investor could theoretically buy or sell tokenized Nvidia on a Sunday afternoon, even though Nvidia's shares aren't trading on Nasdaq at the time. Prices on the new venue would be determined by buying and selling in its own pools rather than simply being set by the latest Nasdaq price.
The market also won't be open to anonymous users. Investors will have to undergo identity and anti-money-laundering checks before they can trade, according to the filing.
So while the investment may look familiar — a token backed by a share of Nvidia, with dividends and voting rights — the machinery underneath it is very different.
Whether investors embrace it is another question, however.
TD Securities said it still sees “limited near-term relevance for institutional investors,” in part because U.S. investors already have efficient access to listed stocks. It also pointed to weak interest from companies in having their shares tokenized and uncertainty around the regulatory framework.
The SEC relief lasts five years rather than establishing permanent rules, which could make large financial firms reluctant to spend money connecting their systems to a market whose regulatory foundation could later change.
The bigger test will be whether enough investors and trading firms participate to keep prices closely aligned with the traditional stocks, particularly overnight and on weekends when U.S. exchanges are closed.
However, the venue will be the first test of appetite from both traders and corporations for tokenized stocks. ""The biggest takeaway is NOT that OKXICE is launching another tokenized-stock venue,"" said Harvey Li, founder of Tokenization Insight.
""It is that OKXICE could become one of the first real-world tests of which tokenization model can scale U.S. equities onchain the fastest, and ultimately become the dominant market structure.""
As stablecoins move into regulated finance, APAC is becoming a key proving ground. This report maps the region’s rules, use cases, and RLUSD’s role.
As stablecoins move into regulated finance, APAC is becoming a key proving ground. This report maps the region’s rules, use cases, and RLUSD’s role.
Why it matters:
As stablecoins move into regulated finance, APAC is becoming a key proving ground. This report maps the region’s rules, use cases, and RLUSD’s role.

> Zero to Agent in 30 Minutes
Turn existing APIs into tools an AI assistant can use


By Michelle Smith October 5, 2026 • 3 minute read
Developers already have useful capabilities exposed through REST APIs. The Model Context Protocol (MCP) lets developers make those capabilities available to AI clients without rebuilding the underlying application.
In this episode of Zero to Agent in 30 Minutes, Bruce Hopkins, an AI developer, author, and longtime software educator, shows how to do that with an MCP server. His demo wraps an existing stock-data API in an MCP server so an MCP client can call it.
You don’t need to replace the systems that already handle your application logic to help them work with agents. You can add an MCP interface around existing capabilities to give an AI client a standard way to discover and use them. Be sure to check out Bruce’s GitHub repo for working code you can adapt for your own APIs.
Next week, AI engineer Sajal Sharma returns to Zero to Agent in 30 Minutes to build a personal assistant on OpenClaw. He’ll show how an agent can keep tasks and notes in Markdown, run proactive automations, and deliver scheduled updates such as a regular morning briefing without waiting for a new prompt.
Follow along with Zero to Agent in 30 Minutes on Radar, or watch the latest episode on YouTube, Spotify, Apple, or wherever you get your podcasts. If you’re an O’Reilly member, you can watch live. Save your seat.
