57,2026-10-05,"A group of neatly dressed young people gathered in the auditorium of the Heritage Foundation headquarters this summer to observe the cherished rituals of the D.C. internship. Most of the girls had long skirts and updos; the boys wore suits that didn’t quite fit—still trying Washington on for size. From a low stage, Kevin Roberts, the president of Heritage, bantered with them about the constants of D.C. life: their softball team, the ice-cream social scheduled for that afternoon. Then he launched into a professorial discourse about the nature of conservatism. The interns scribbled intently in Heritage notebooks, branded with the Liberty Bell.
For decades, a very specific kind of right-wing kid has dreamed about working at Heritage. “I would look at the building in awe,” a former member of the management team told me. The organization has an annual budget of about a hundred million dollars, and more than four hundred million in reserves. Its properties take up an entire city block on the Senate side of the Capitol. Heritage is the undisputed giant of the conservative-think-tank world—less a tank than an aircraft carrier.
When Roberts took over as leader, five years ago, he was eager to adopt an aggressive posture. Suddenly, managers were speaking in sports metaphors (We’re going to move the football!) or martial ones (We’re here to do battle!). A longtime Heritage scholar named Mike Gonzalez told me, earnestly, “What I say about Kevin—it’s kind of like watching ‘Braveheart’ every morning.”
Roberts is tall and bald, with the permanently gregarious manner of someone who, in another life, could have been a politician. Several people described him to me as a man’s man: he drives a black diesel pickup and wears cowboy boots to formal functions. When the Guardian reported old allegations that he had killed a neighbor’s noisy dog, he took to making jokes about knowing how to use a shovel (though he denies killing the dog). He is skilled at selling Heritage to donors, and also at cultivating right-wing staffers in Washington. Lately, however, the latter crowd has been causing him trouble.
As one former Heritage executive put it, the young people who came to D.C. used to be the good kids—teachers’ pets, class presidents, suck-ups. Now the right-wing dreamers deride their elders as “Reagan Zombies” and “BoomerCons.” They’re sick of the establishment program of forever wars and free trade, open borders and kowtowing to China. Their movement—known as the New Right, or sometimes as the Realignment—is virulently antiwar, eager for mass deportations, and less interested in lowering corporate taxes than in helping families afford houses and cars and kids. “It’s Elizabeth Warren in the streets, economic nationalism and strong Christianity in the sheets,” one D.C. think-tanker told me. The kids these days don’t watch Hannity; they follow Tucker, or maybe Nick Fuentes, the young white nationalist who mocks women and calls Hitler “cool.” “Everyone in the mainstream on the right is puzzled about what to do about the angry young men,” a former Heritage scholar told me. “It creates nasty fractures in the coalition.”
An intern at the event this summer asked Roberts what he thought about Carlson and Fuentes. “Never heard of them,” Roberts joked, to laughter. Then he acknowledged that their appeal was something “we have tried to grapple with here.” He said that it was a reaction to “what is perceived to be the inauthenticity of institutions, that institutions can’t be trusted.” He mentioned colleges, churches, and the government—not think tanks, but perhaps it was implied. “The way some people, a few people, make sense of that is a burn-it-all-down mentality,” he said.
Once the midterms have passed, the conservative movement will have to reckon with its impending post-Trump reality. Different factions want vastly different futures—and, in recent years, these factions have been fighting for control of Heritage. One important dividing line is whether you’re under or over the age of forty-five. Roberts’s hope is to influence the next generation: to disciple them, to direct their anger, to correct their excesses. “This is a civilizational moment for this republic,” he told me recently. “Whatever battles exist out there, whether they’re political battles, ideological battles—God forbid, more real battles—let them be in my lifetime, right now, so my kids don’t have to fight them.”
Heritage was founded in the nineteen-seventies—an age of relative institutional stability, when it was taken for granted that experts could shape history. For decades, it was the clubhouse of the conservative movement, and its alumni took powerful positions across D.C. “It’s almost like we were missionaries,” one former staffer told me. “We were so driven by the belief that what we were doing was important and meaningful.” In 2010, the organization launched Heritage Action for America, a lobbying group that enforced ideological purity on Capitol Hill by shaming apostate Republicans. “There was a consensus, in the 2000-to-2012 era, of what principled conservatism stood for,” Tim Chapman, a former head of Heritage Action, told me. It was more or less the Heritage mission statement: limited government, free enterprise, individual freedom, traditional values, and a strong national defense.
When Donald Trump gained political prominence, in 2016, Heritage leaders recognized his potential—yet the organization slumped during his first term. Kay Coles James, who took over the foundation in 2018, was an uninspiring fund-raiser, and her politics were, as one senior think-tank leader put it, “bog-standard Republican establishment.” During the protests of 2020, James, who is Black, called racism “a fatal wound” in America’s history. Tucker Carlson helped lead a push for her ouster.
The board of trustees wanted Heritage to be relevant again, and Roberts seemed to offer MAGA credibility. He had been the president of a Catholic college in Wyoming and the head of the Texas Public Policy Foundation—an “America First” guy who understood the grass roots. He came up in the populist wing of the Republican Party; as a young man in Louisiana, he organized a rally for Pat Buchanan, the nationalist Presidential candidate. For a time, Roberts changed course, cheering for the Iraq War, but now he describes himself as a “recovering neocon.”
The details of Roberts’s policy preferences weren’t necessarily that important to Heritage’s supporters. The foundation has a lot of small and mid-tier donors: the Midwestern plumber who sold his business to private equity, or “retired parents who got involved because of their woke grandchild’s public school,” as one former Heritage analyst said. They know what they believe: Reagan was good. Free markets are good. Trump is good. They do not follow baroque conflicts online, or “sit around and think about the ultimate questions,” the former member of Heritage’s management team said. As long as you could mail out pictures of Roberts with Trump, it seemed, the donations would keep flowing.
D.C. Republicans, however, were watching Roberts for signs of his true loyalties. One of the leading New Right groups is National Conservatism, or NatCon. After taking over, Roberts declared at a NatCon conference, “I come not to invite National Conservatives to join our conservative movement but to acknowledge the plain truth, that Heritage is already part of yours.” His book “Dawn’s Early Light,” from 2024, reads like a tribute to the Party’s restless young populists, full of scornful references to “wax-museum conservatives.” He insists that American life has become rigged against working people. “The U.S. economy today bears the same resemblance to a system of free enterprise as a movie set does to a real stock exchange,” he writes.
Link copied
In 2023, Roberts invited Carlson to give the keynote speech at Heritage’s fiftieth-anniversary gala. Carlson spoke on the importance of political bravery. “You look with disdain and sadness as you see people you know become quislings,” he said. “You see them going along with the new new thing, which is clearly a poisonous thing, a silly thing, saying things you know they don’t believe, because they want to keep their jobs.”
For all Roberts’s populist leanings, he has been preoccupied with restoring Heritage’s role as the convener of the conservative movement. “One of the big themes that haunts Dr. Roberts is the big tent,” a former researcher at the foundation told me. A Trump Administration official with close ties to Roberts said that Heritage was roughly divided into the same factions as the rest of the conservative movement. There were the MAGA types, who were inclined toward nationalism and cultural conservatism. There was the wing known as MIGA, a dismissive shorthand for Make Israel Great Again: scholars who favored a hawkish, pro-Israel foreign policy. And there were the Freedom Conservatives, libertarians with a religious streak.
Roberts tried to appease them all. After Hamas attacked Israel in 2023, for example, he agreed to let Heritage host a new conservative task force on antisemitism. But taming the organization proved difficult. Heritage shared the unruly qualities of a liberal-arts college: legacy fiefdoms, ornery academics, outright defiance of authority. Scholars guarded their territory fiercely; there aren’t many jobs that pay conservative intellectuals generously to write books and argue about ideas. To Heritage veterans, it was obvious that the institution’s president should defer to in-house experts. But that left Roberts trying to coax nearly four hundred employees into alignment. “There are not a lot of people who are loyal to the management,” a second former staffer told me. “They’re loyal to the brand of Heritage.”
The organization’s culture involves a great deal of process fetish, a predilection more closely associated with socialists than with suit-coat conservatives. The most telling example is its “one voice” policy. Traditionally, when Heritage was preparing to put out a paper or take a position, staff would assemble in a conference room, where the lead authors would present their view and then get hammered with questions. The ritual could be gruelling—occasionally, there was shouting—but, once Heritage took a position, no one at the organization was allowed to criticize it in public. In previous eras, “there would be one or two, maybe three positions that would come out in a year that I didn’t like,” John Malcolm, a former head of Heritage’s legal center, told me. After Roberts arrived, Malcolm started disagreeing with two or three a month.
Roberts pushed Heritage to promote a pro-family culture, dignified work, an America First national-security policy, and “a virtuous, patriotic citizenry.” He was particularly preoccupied by the declining birth rate. “A family should not be a luxury good,” he writes in his book. He asked Heritage scholars for a major paper on how the government could encourage people to get married and have kids.
The cultural conservatives in the domestic-policy office loved the idea, but the free-market economists were resistant. Richard Stern, who was then the head of the econ office, was charged with mediating between the teams. There were endless meetings, which often devolved into freshman-dorm-level debates about first principles. Stern told me that some of his colleagues “view the ultimate goal of human life as to serve some kind of collective,” adding, “I really can’t justify spending my time and effort serving collectivist ends.” Roberts grew impatient. A former high-level Heritage employee recalled a tense meeting: “Kevin slams his hand down on the table, and he says, ‘Give me my fucking policy paper!’ And Richard Stern just sort of sits there, smiling, and proceeds to tell him all the reasons why he wasn’t going to.”
As the internal resistance deepened, the circle around Roberts grew tighter. Many people left or were fired. The legal center, which leaned toward free-market ideas, had its budget frozen and was forbidden to replace employees who left. A former policy staffer recalled going to work on a Monday morning and finding that a handful of colleagues were simply gone—casualties of the campaign to eliminate what one executive called the Heritage deep state.
Since the Reagan years, Heritage has proposed policies for each potential Republican Administration. For the 2024 election cycle, Roberts envisioned a sprawling initiative called Project 2025. The goal was to unite the conservative movement; more than a hundred organizations eventually contributed. The plan grew to include a database of prospective hires and some nine hundred pages of policy proposals—but its ambition ended up being a liability. “It was a great, unifying project,” a former Heritage staffer who now works at a competing shop said. “Yet it was obnoxiously branded in the public with the bell.” Roberts aggressively advertised his supposed influence in Trumpworld, saying that Heritage was “institutionalizing Trumpism” and guiding such efforts as abolishing the Department of Education and deporting illegal migrants. At some point, Democrats decided that Project 2025 made a great campaign pitch for their side. Kamala Harris called it “a plan that would return America to a very dark past.” Trump distanced himself, claiming, “I know nothing about Project 2025.”
After Trump was reëlected, Heritage found itself in a difficult position—because of Project 2025, and also because Roberts had supported Ron DeSantis early in the Presidential primary. As think tanks jockeyed to place people in the Administration, Heritage seemed marginalized. “I’m sure we got résumés from them,” a former senior White House official told me. But “arm’s length, at best.”
Heritage wasn’t entirely aligned with the new Administration on policy. Roberts was resolutely pro-life, and Trump had backed away from a federal abortion ban. Heritage had also become a vocal opponent of Big Tech, even as tech C.E.O.s sat onstage at Trump’s Inauguration. “Kevin and the leadership at Heritage really decided that, look, President Trump is President,” a conservative leader told me. “To be on the team, you’re not allowed to criticize Donald Trump.”
All this internecine disagreement was largely invisible to outsiders. Thanks in part to Democratic messaging, liberals had come to see Heritage as the malign force behind the new Administration. A comedian from North Carolina named Cliff Cash started recording earnest YouTube videos arguing that Trump had stolen the election. “Project 2025 is really sinister,” he told me. “I haven’t read the entire thing. But, when you look at the policies that real Americans hate the most right now, it’s all from the Heritage Foundation.”
Cash began assembling protesters at Heritage’s headquarters, in what became weekly gatherings. Employees recalled that demonstrators used racial slurs, insulted a staffer’s weight, and even told one young woman, as she headed to Mass, that they hoped she got raped in the ass by a cactus. Cash filmed himself trailing a young man in khakis, shouting, “Looks like we’ve got another pretty-boy Nazi fascist.” Cash told me, “I don’t have any grace or patience or forgiveness for anyone working there. I don’t care if they’re twenty-eight or they run the fax machine.”
On protest days, Heritage arranged for black S.U.V.s to shuttle staff through the crowd. Roberts rallied employees by blasting “The Star-Spangled Banner” at the demonstrators. Inside the building, the protests created a sense of unity. “Nothing brings people together more than this feeling of us versus them,” the former policy staffer told me. But the agitators outside also distracted from the divisions inside. “When everyone is screaming at you, ‘You’re all Nazis,’ you don’t look around at people in the building who you don’t know very well and be, like, ‘So what do you think of Nick Fuentes?’ ” the staffer said. “It turns out, maybe we should have.”
In October, Carlson invited Fuentes to his home in Maine. The two had been feuding, after Fuentes accused Carlson’s father of being in the C.I.A. Now they recorded a podcast episode in Carlson’s barn, under an eighteenth-century Scottish deer-antler chandelier, and afterward they posed for a photo with Carlson’s arm draped paternally around Fuentes’s shoulder. The backlash was immediate across the conservative movement. “No to the groypers,” the podcast host Ben Shapiro wrote on X. “No to cowards like Tucker Carlson, who normalize their trash.”
Heritage didn’t have an obvious connection with the controversy. Earlier that year, though, it had signed an advertising contract with Carlson, who had agreed to read out potted endorsements on air. (“It’s not just thinking, it’s doing, and if you want to support it go to Heritage dot-org slash Tucker.”) By the time of the Fuentes interview, the deal had expired, but no one at Heritage had removed the page promoting the partnership. As criticism grew, someone in donor relations panicked and took the page down. A few sharp observers called Heritage out on X for distancing itself from Carlson.
The official narrative of what happened next—which has been the subject of furious debate in Washington—hinges on a man named Ryan Neuhaus, Roberts’s chief of staff. Neuhaus is a devout Mormon who got married while at Brigham Young University and later became a staffer for the Utah senator Mike Lee; now, in his early thirties, he has four children. “He’s a big-brother figure to younger kids in the Realignment,” the former researcher told me. Behind the scenes, though, he also has a reputation for scrapping. One former Republican Senate staffer told me, “His view was that the hard-line approach was always right.” When Neuhaus joined Heritage, in 2025, he showed little patience for those who resisted the new regime. He liked to respond to critiques by quoting aloud from Roberts’s book.
After the Fuentes interview, Neuhaus started seeing criticism of Heritage online and “got Kevin riled up,” according to the second former staffer. In a meeting the next morning, Neuhaus told senior staff that the foundation should make a statement. Some were confused about why that was necessary, but they agreed to have Neuhaus propose language for approval. Instead, according to leaders at Heritage, he wrote a draft and took it directly to the foundation’s video-production team. Roberts recorded a video, and it went online.
For those who hadn’t been following the Carlson-Fuentes controversy, the video was likely hard to parse. “I want to be clear about one thing: Christians can critique the State of Israel without being antisemitic,” Roberts tells the camera. “Of course, antisemitism should be condemned.” But, he adds, conservatives shouldn’t have to support a foreign government, no matter “how loud” the “mouthpieces” of the “globalist class” are in Washington. “We will always defend our friends against the slander of bad actors who serve someone else’s agenda,” he says. “That includes Tucker Carlson.” He condemns “the venomous coalition” that is “sowing division” by attacking Carlson. He abhors Fuentes’s ideas, he adds, “but cancelling him is not the answer.”
At first, New Right leaders circulated the video approvingly. “Kevin’s a leader for this moment, and I’ve never been more excited about where our movement is headed,” Nick Solheim, the C.E.O. of American Moment, which trains conservative staffers, wrote on X. Curt Mills, the editor of The American Conservative, told me that the video was “a forthright statement in defense of free speech” and an avowal that “anti-Zionism is not ipso facto antisemitism.”
Others on the right thought that Carlson had gone too easy on Fuentes, and that Roberts had covered for him. Some found Roberts’s language to be rife with antisemitic tropes: “globalist class,” “venomous coalition.” Still others felt that the response had been strategically dumb. “It was like killing a gnat with a sledgehammer,” the former staffer who’s now at a competing shop said.
In my conversations with right-wingers who remain angry about Roberts’s video, though, the most prevalent concern was about Carlson himself. On his podcast, he focusses relentlessly on Israel’s sins and has interviewed Holocaust deniers. “Tucker is the leading promoter of antisemitism on the right,” Yoram Hazony, a founder of the NatCon movement, told me. “This isn’t about foreign policy. It’s about causing people to hate Jews.”
At Heritage, the leadership was surprised by the intensity of the reaction. Roberts put out another lengthy statement on X. “Nick Fuentes’s antisemitism is not complicated, ironic, or misunderstood,” he said. “It is explicit, dangerous, and demands our unified opposition.” The replies were divided between trollish memes and a repeated question from movement conservatives: Thanks, but what about Tucker?
As the argument festered, a Heritage tax expert named Preston Brashers tweeted a Norman Rockwell painting of a lone dissenter in a town meeting, and captioned it “NAZIS ARE BAD.” Brashers was nervous about speaking out—“I was shaking as I tweeted,” he told me—but colleagues supported him. “Almost half a million Americans died defending this very simple premise,” Amy Swearer, a Second Amendment expert, chimed in. On the other side, Brian Darling, a lobbyist who used to work at Heritage, tweeted that “virtue signalling employees” should resign, adding that their departure would be “addition by subtraction.” Neuhaus retweeted this.
Link copied
The conflict had transformed from a fight over a tweet about a podcast to a replay of the supreme moral conflict of the twentieth century. Nearly everyone on the board recognized the need to act, according to one former member. Roberts called Neuhaus and confronted him. Within days, Neuhaus had resigned.
Soon afterward, Roberts spoke about the episode at an internal town hall. “I made a mistake, and I let you down,” he said. “And I am sorry for that. Period. Full stop.” He confessed to not knowing that much about Fuentes. “I actually don’t have time to consume a lot of news,” he said. “I consume a lot of sports.” Yet, even as he gestured at magnanimity—“The buck stops on the desk in my office”—he didn’t entirely accept the blame. “When the script was presented to me, I understood from our former colleague that it was approved,” he said. The implication was clear: Neuhaus had lied, and he had to go.
Many on the right seemed happy to accept this story. Josh Hammer, a conservative commentator, told me that the video “was dripping with such blatant antisemitic overtones that it really does raise the obvious question as to how no one had realized this. Or was it actually the intention of the individual who wrote it, Ryan Neuhaus?”
But at certain happy hours, in certain group chats, a different story emerged. In this version, Roberts’s phone had been deluged after the Fuentes interview with messages pushing him to respond, and he wanted to make a statement immediately. Although Heritage leaders later suggested that Neuhaus went rogue to make that happen, several people told me the organization was too tediously bureaucratic for that to be possible. “There was no way for a product like this to move without significant review,” the former researcher said. I heard from a number of people that senior leaders had been on a Signal chat, editing the script and then vetting the final video.
Within the New Right, the situation came to be viewed as something like a coup attempt. Mike Howell, a visiting scholar at Heritage who also runs a group called the Oversight Project, told me, “It was about the Republican establishment attempting to take back Heritage, because they had lost it.” The grandest theory was that it was a manifestation of the fight over the Presidential nomination in 2028: Carlson had been an early supporter of J. D. Vance, and so the attacks on Heritage were really an attack on Vance and his allies. Among leaders of right-wing institutions in Washington, the senior think-tank leader added, “there was an understanding that it was very important for Kevin to survive this.”
Several people told me that Neuhaus signed an N.D.A. before leaving; he was a young father with a mortgage, and he needed the severance. But that meant the official version of events was left without rebuttal. Especially in the New Right’s young-guy group chats, this was an epochal event. “It lingered for months of us talking about how much we hate Heritage,” the former researcher told me. “I will not go to a Heritage event ever again.”
Despite the controversy, Roberts never took the video down, and he never actually disavowed Carlson. The number of Heritage’s media appearances plummeted, from more than a hundred a week to a handful. A kind of paralysis set in among the trustees, the former board member said. Six left, including Abby Spencer Moffat, an heiress whose family had given generously to Heritage.
Members of the antisemitism task force that Roberts had approved decided to break ties with the foundation. One Jewish donor told me that he had originally supported Heritage because it framed antisemitism as incompatible with the American way of life. But, he said, “anyone that supports Tucker I cannot associate with.”
The fiercest critics of Heritage were those who had spent years waging ideological war within the organization. Former staffers recall that, at one meeting, Robert Rector, a longtime anti-welfare expert, said, “I don’t know why we have all these little Nazis around.” Emma Waters, a staffer in her twenties, shut her laptop and walked out, choking back tears. (Rector, who was summoned to H.R. afterward, says that he called only one person a Nazi, and that he was joking.)
Stern, the economist, started quietly talking with colleagues about leaving, and so did Malcolm, the head of the legal department. When Heritage found out, it fired Malcolm and one of his deputies. Stern mounted a mass exodus: fourteen people, constituting almost three full departments.
All of them had arranged new jobs at Advancing American Freedom, a small think tank founded by the former Vice-President Mike Pence after the events of January 6th put him at odds with much of his party. In December, 2025, alone, A.A.F. raised fifteen million dollars to hire unhappy Heritage staffers. When I visited its headquarters, the atmosphere was triumphant, at least for an office. On one wall, a giant screen displayed media appearances by Malcolm and by Swearer, one of the “NAZIS ARE BAD” tweeters.
Pence wasn’t around, but his office was convincingly staged: framed family portraits, a heavy wooden desk, a Windsor chair from the White House. Tim Chapman, the former Heritage Action leader, is now the president of A.A.F., and I met him in his office across the hall. He’s forty-eight, but his décor evoked an earlier time, with posters of Calvin Coolidge and The Saturday Evening Post. “There’s an institutional impulse, always, to be perceived as hip, or on the cutting edge,” he told me. “We’re overweighting the influence of young people.” He pointed to a poster behind his desk of “Ronbo”—Ronald Reagan’s head superimposed on the body of Sylvester Stallone. “At some point I was, like, You know what, if they’re going to call me a Zombie Reaganite, lean in,” he said.
During the first Trump Administration, there were well-publicized battles between MAGA and the Never Trumpers. Chapman wasn’t really part of those squabbles. After the events of January 6th, though, he rejected what he saw as a growing attitude on the right: that “power politics was the way to go if you wanted to save the country.”
Howell, the Heritage visiting fellow, refers to A.A.F. as “Mike Pence’s retirement home.” The organization is generally viewed with skepticism by movement conservatives. “I wish the mass exit of people didn’t go to Mike Pence,” the former member of Heritage’s management team told me. “The best and the brightest of the conservative movement are saying Heritage is too New Right,” but their argument was undermined “because it was Pence, and he has such a mixed reputation.”
A.A.F. has picked fights with the President, but they’re not the ones you’d expect from the Never Trump crowd. During the 2024 Republican National Convention, when the Party downplayed its opposition to abortion, A.A.F. pushed for a more pro-life stance. The effort was unsuccessful, but A.A.F. demonstrated that it was willing to brawl. In some ways, its existence proves that it’s still possible to fund-raise off the hope that the G.O.P. will return to a previous version of itself, and that the think-tank ecosystem can thrive without any obvious policy results. “We’re not trying to project to everybody that we’re having meetings over at 1600 Pennsylvania on a weekly basis,” Chapman said. “You have to be really realistic about where we are right now. Principled conservatives are not in the driver’s seat of the Republican Party.”
After Carlson interviewed Fuentes, he retreated to his hunting camp and disconnected from the outside world. About twelve hours later, though, he checked his phone and saw a supportive text from Roberts: I will never forsake you. Carlson told me that the two of them were friendly, but not exactly friends; they had gone hunting together once. “It’s not his job to stand up for me,” Carlson said. But then, “the next thing I know, he’s taking it all back. And I felt like, Boy, I wish I’d talked to him.”
Carlson is the embodiment of a certain ethos on the right: a wartime mentality of trusting your trench mates and ceding no ground. He maintained that Roberts should have stuck to his initial statement, saying, in essence: “ ‘Look, I don’t agree with everything Tucker Carlson says, but he’s my friend. And, moreover, if you don’t like what he said, let’s debate it. We’re not going to cancel people.’ ” Carlson told me, “If he’d said that and gotten fired by the board anyway, he would have had a lot of opportunities to go from there, and a lot of money to fund them with. Cowardice is punished. And that’s what that was. That was cowardice.”
Carlson famously got ejected from Fox News—and thus from the conservative establishment—in 2023. Since then, he has been free to pursue his apparent obsessions, including the existence of demons, the evils of Christian Zionism, and Vladimir Putin. He has also become a fierce critic of institutional conservatism; this summer, he announced that he is leaving the G.O.P. “The academic guys, they’re always quoting Churchill, Roosevelt, ‘The Man in the Arena,’ ” Carlson said. “They speak in martial language, which is always a tip that they have no experience of violence.” Heritage, he said, exists primarily to sustain itself: “It’s an electric windmill. It’s a self-licking ice-cream cone.”
The drama around Heritage nearly obscured the interview that inspired it. The episode goes on for two hours, and Carlson spends the first half gently teasing out Fuentes’s story and his sense of grievance. At several points, Carlson disavows antisemitism (“Going on about the Jews helps the neocons”), but he doesn’t ask Fuentes to do so. This outraged listeners, but Carlson told me that his approach was strategic, a way of getting a reflexive troll like Fuentes to engage. He pointed to an interview that Piers Morgan did with Fuentes around the same time. “Fuentes is faster, because he’s younger—that’s just biological,” he said. “But he also has a grasp of postmodern irony that Piers doesn’t. Piers is, like, You question the Holocaust, and Nick goes, ‘Well, is it too soon?’ ” Carlson argued that, if he’d confronted Fuentes, the interview would have devolved into spectacle. “It’s pretty easy to get suckered into a situation where you devalue your own case, sap your own moral authority, and basically look like some geezer lecturing the kids on how hip-hop is evil.”
For Carlson, the more important exchange comes toward the end of the conversation. Fuentes is going on about how women are “frumpy” and “obnoxious” and “overweight.” Women, he argues, are “very simple.”
“Have you ever lived with one?” Carlson says. Fuentes admits that he has not—and then listens, seemingly intent, while Carlson explains the virtues of actual relationships.
Carlson told me that he didn’t press Fuentes on his admiration of Hitler and Stalin because he thinks Fuentes is not credible when he talks about history. But, he said, if Fuentes’s young male fans think that “women are all bitches, or sluts, or whatever, that’s way more real than, like, your opinions on Hamas.”
Carlson’s network claims that his podcast episodes draw an average audience of fifty-six million across platforms. His fans don’t necessarily care that he hosts offensive guests, or that he has reversed some of the opinions he previously expressed on Fox; his willingness to violate the pieties of mainstream media is part of his appeal. In a low-trust environment, Carlson said, “it’s O.K. to be wrong. But any kind of lying or deceit or cloaked motives or falseness is just not acceptable.”
One night in D.C., I had dinner with Chris DeMuth, who spent two decades as the president of the American Enterprise Institute. He picked the restaurant: Ocean Prime, an expense-account spot with leather booths, where the cheapest steak on the dinner menu is sixty-six dollars. DeMuth, who is eighty, seems like precisely the kind of old-school Washington type young conservatives mock these days. Instead, he’s spent the last stretch of his career as an unlikely leader of the New Right.
Over dinner, DeMuth described becoming disenchanted with ideas that he’d advocated for decades. “I would have told you, ‘What we want is free trade and globalization, and we’ll all get rich, and we’ll take that money and we’ll spend it on job training for men who have been thrown out of work,’ ” he said. “I had these little talking points. But they didn’t really lead anywhere.” He grew more concerned about what he saw as the erosion of American culture, with mass migration, the hollowing out of communities, and the weakening of marriage and gender norms. “Young people look at the old people and say, ‘Look at the world you left us,’ ” he told me. “They think conservatives didn’t fight hard enough. And I’ve come to the view that they’re right.”
People across the political spectrum feel that parts of adult life that used to be taken for granted—a house, a spouse, stable employment, children—are now out of reach. The former Republican Senate staffer told me that there’s a pervasive feeling among his young colleagues on the right: “You’ve been lied to.” Received political dogmas ring hollow; instead, young conservative intellectuals are reaching for traditional faith, or for transformative legislative agendas. There’s a lot of worry in Washington about the extent to which the right has been infiltrated by Catholic monarchists and Fuentes devotees. But even less radical young people are grappling with who they can trust and what they believe.
Link copied
Hazony, the NatCon leader, told me that conservatives “find themselves in the crossfire between a genuinely anti-Jewish, antisemitic movement in one corner of the Party and a pro-Jewish, pro-Zionist wing that is often overreaching in its rhetoric.” He sees Heritage, inflected by Roberts’s populist sensibilities, as crucial for guiding the movement to “where the majority of the Republican Party is”: they “want to see a rejuvenated American nationalism which is not bigoted and hostile to important communities.” Hazony, an Orthodox Jew who lives in Jerusalem, remains concerned about antisemitism on the right. But he resists panicking that Gen Z antisemites will take over. “I don’t think it’s anywhere near time to give up on that generation of Republicans,” he said. “There’s a very good chance that Tucker is going to lose.”
Soon after the Heritage video came out, Hazony contacted Roberts and offered to help. The two have a long-standing relationship; Roberts has called Hazony “my closest Jewish friend in the world.” In the past year, Hazony said, people at Heritage have come to “understand what their role is, which is to hold together and build out the Protestant-Catholic-Jewish alliance at the center of the Party.”
This spring, I visited Roberts at Heritage. His office was the size of a comfortable studio apartment, with cream-colored drapes and walls the hue of orange wine. Hints of Roberts’s Texas background poked through the formality: a cowhide pillow on the couch, a giant Alamo flag over the desk. Roberts, wearing his signature cowboy boots, greeted me with practiced warmth, but he was unmistakably nervous. Not long into my visit, his assistant poked her head in, to make sure he didn’t want to bail on the interview.
When I’d spoken to Heritage staffers, they’d frequently used the words “move on” about the video. But it was clearly on Roberts’s mind. He brought up “videogate” before I did. “Obviously, I stepped in it,” he said. I asked why, after all these months, Heritage still hadn’t taken the clip down. “I’m a historian,” he said. And, besides, “if I were to take it down, it would still exist.”
Roberts acknowledged a larger problem. “There’s a very troubling rise of antisemitism on the right,” he said, adding that he intends to “really make sure Heritage continues to be a vital institutional player in that work against antisemitism.” In think-tank fashion, the organization has initiated studies on the origins of right-wing antisemitism, the first of which is expected this month; it plans to hold events and possibly propose legislation around the findings. Roberts told me that he had called up the presidents of Catholic colleges—he wouldn’t tell me which ones—and “privately excoriated” them for hiring professors he sees as antisemitic. This spring, Heritage hired Luke Moon, a Christian pastor who co-chaired the antisemitism task force, as a visiting fellow. When I asked Roberts whether he had been persuaded that Carlson is exacerbating antisemitism in America, he smiled, as though he had been waiting for the question. “Yes,” he said.
The consensus in Washington is that Heritage has been weakened by the events of the past few years. Although its fund-raising e-mails describe sweeping influence—“The Trump administration has already implemented the majority of our policy recommendations”—the former senior White House official dismissed these claims as “preposterous on every level.” He added, “They’re not coming in and doing briefings. No one’s paying attention to what they’re saying.”
Yet a number of insiders argued that the crisis consolidated Roberts’s power. The senior think-tank leader told me, “The low-key win in all of this for Kevin is now he gets to remake everything.” This spring and summer, Heritage announced a series of big hires, and in May the board gained four new members, all allies of Roberts: Hazony, two businessmen, and Mollie Hemingway, the editor-in-chief of the conservative publication The Federalist. The board seems to be sticking by Roberts. “This is a hard job, and he’ll probably make other mistakes,” Larry Arnn, the president of Hillsdale College, who is a trustee, told me. “He will do what Churchill said: proceed from failure to failure to ultimate success.”
In December, Heritage quietly débuted what it calls its Cornerstone principles. The foundation has always issued grandly named bullet points summarizing its agenda. But the Cornerstones downplay many of its longtime watchwords—individual liberty, limited government, free enterprise. Instead, they emphasize the “civilizational” questions that Roberts is interested in.
After years of delay, Roberts’s family-policy paper came out in January. Heritage scholars proposed a marriage “bootcamp” for cohabiting couples, government-seeded investment accounts with a special benefit for those who marry before the age of thirty, and child-care tax credits for married stay-at-home parents, something that Vance has recently championed. Roberts said that the paper demonstrated a restoration of the one-voice policy. He told me, “When we say, ‘This is one of our priorities,’ you may not work against that priority while you’re at Heritage. And that was happening, and that’s not tolerated here.”
Roberts told me that he is trying to use his influence in Washington differently now. “I learned late last year I don’t have to comment on everything,” he said. “There can be real power in speaking truth to power—whispering.” Unlike many right-wing populists, Roberts has completely backed Trump’s war in Iran. “No president in the 47-year history of the Islamic Republic has been willing to take it on like President Trump,” he tweeted in June. (Last month, Trump hailed him as “a fabulous guy.”)
Heritage is already working on Project 2029, and the staff sent me some promising fund-raising numbers: Roberts, they say, raised ninety-seven million dollars in 2025, outperforming previous years. Yet the foundation still has to contend with the increasingly uncertain role of think tanks in Washington. Ideas travel differently now; it’s not clear that panel discussions and policy briefs actually drive the political agenda. There’s a lot of focus on influencers as the true source of political power. Eric Teetsel, a former Heritage executive, told me that “think tanks need to think of themselves more like management companies.” The podcast may be the white paper of our time.
Not everyone wants to entrust the movement to social-media personalities. Chapman, the head of A.A.F., told me, “The correction for today’s craziness is for people to go and look for institutions that they trust.” Delano Squires, a Heritage scholar whom Roberts described to me as a future face of the right, offered a similarly institutionalist view. “Among the influencer class, I’ve definitely seen the embrace of a robust white-identity politics that has served as a counterweight to what people would call woke politics,” he said. But Squires, who is Black, argued that a lot of the toxicity on the right lives exclusively online. “Exercising discernment in how we engage in the influencer class is a sign of wisdom,” he said.
Institutions like Heritage face a puzzle. They want the kind of audience loyalty that someone like Carlson generates—yet much of Carlson’s appeal lies in being anti-institutional. Roberts told me that Heritage’s solution to the problem of “solid, faithful, daily-Mass-attending guys listening to nonsense” is to create “better things to follow and to watch and to listen to.” Heritage hopes to cultivate its own internet stars, by using in-house experts to move public opinion. “My job is to help build a platform for those people,” he told me. The specifics are still a little vague, though—“that’s what we’re trying to figure out,” he said.
During the intern event at Heritage, Roberts called for an incremental approach to winning over the next generation. Ultimately, he said, America will change as its people change—as citizens live lives of virtue, build families, lift their communities. This kind of plan is “not quick enough for the people who want to burn it all down,” he acknowledged. But Heritage wants to persuade young people “that there’s hope ahead.” He showed only the slightest hint of uncertainty: “Let’s say that we at Heritage are wrong about that.” About the possibility of hope, or of persuading young people, it wasn’t clear. “We’re still going to do the same thing,” he said. “What other option is there?” ♦

58,2026-10-05,"The smartwatch market often forces a compromise: you either choose a full-featured daily driver that needs to sit on a charger every night or a dedicated fitness tracker that lacks meaningful smart capabilities. Most users end up picking their poison, trading seamless notifications for battery life or rich athletic metrics for apps.
Motorola is stepping back into the ring to eliminate that compromise with the launch of the Moto Watch Ultra (2026). Priced at $349.99, it combines Google’s Wear OS ecosystem with performance tracking “Powered by Polar” aiming squarely at users who want serious fitness intelligence wrapped in a classic lifestyle design.
At first glance, the Moto Watch Ultra doesn’t look like a typical plasticky sport watch. Motorola opted for a premium 46.2mm stainless-steel case that strikes a clean balance between rugged build quality and refined style. Around back, standard 22mm universal lugs make swapping straps simple, and Motorola conveniently ships the watch with both silicone and leather bands right out of the box.
The front of the watch is dominated by a 1.5-inch 2.5D curved pOLED panel running at a 466×466 resolution, which keeps text sharp and watch faces vibrant. Pushing up to 2,700 nits of peak brightness, the screen remains easily readable even under harsh midday sun during outdoor runs. To handle daily wear and tear, Motorola protected the display with Corning Gorilla Glass 3 alongside IP68 dust and water resistance, supplemented by a 5ATM rating for submersion up to 50 meters.
One thing I’d like to have seen on a model with the Ultra moniker is a sapphire crystal screen instead of the Gorilla Glass. If you’re positioning this as the top tier, rugged model, it needs to have a display that can take a bigger beating than Corning’s offering. Sapphire is tried and true in this space and we’ve even seen it in other Wear OS models like the OnePlus Watch 3.
Rather than building a health suite completely from scratch, Motorola partnered with sports science pioneer Polar to drive its bio-analytics. The integration brings algorithm-backed depth to daily physical monitoring and recovery tracking.
Overnight tracking relies on Polar’s Sleep Plus Stages and Nightly Recharge tools to evaluate sleep architecture alongside autonomic nervous system recovery, giving you a clear picture of how ready your body is for physical strain each morning. For daily stress management, Serene Guided Breathing provides real-time biofeedback across three targeted serenity zones. 
Meanwhile, the watch tracks total energy expenditure through Smart Energy metrics, breaking down the specific ratio of carbohydrates to fats burned during workouts while integrated dual-band GPS maintains a tight satellite lock and precise route mapping through dense tree cover or city streets. 
Overall, the app experience is pretty robust and does have some pull to get former Polar users to possibly consider this Wear OS watch. I like that the Stamina feature shows my general readiness, or “how I should feel score,” similar to Garmin’s Body Battery and AmazFit’s HybridCharge. 
One thing I don’t like is not being able to actively see my HRV metrics. This is a good indicator of bad sleep or even oncoming sickness that just doesn’t have a home on the app. It’s considered for the Stamina and Sleep scores, but I can’t seem to find a dedicated spot to view just those readings.
I do have one request to all Wear OS watches that I think this Moto and Polar marriage can solve. Please give us the option to pair external heart sensors in the UI somewhere. There are movements like barbell front squats and kettlebell power cleans that are literally painful to do with a watch on at heavy weights. Being able to wear a banded sensor either across the chest or bicep is much more comfortable. Polar has this feature with their fitness watches, as does every major competitor, and it’s time this was built into Wear OS. 
The Moto Watch Ultra pairs elite Polar biometrics with full Wear OS capabilities and an ultra-bright 2,700-nit display, striking a well-rounded balance between athletic intelligence and smart daily performance—though power users will still charge daily and may hesitate at the Gorilla Glass display.
Under the hood, the watch is powered by Qualcomm’s Snapdragon W5+ Gen 1 platform, ensuring fast app launches, smooth animations, and solid power efficiency across Wear OS by Google. Motorola has also committed to providing two major OS upgrades alongside two years of quarterly security updates.
With integrated Google Gemini, the watch acts as an active assistant right on your wrist. You can prompt Gemini to summarize incoming notifications, set contextual reminders, or trigger connected smart home routines without reaching for your phone.
Thanks to built-in eSIM support and broad LTE carrier compatibility across AT&T, Verizon, and T-Mobile, the Moto Watch Ultra functions as a completely standalone wearable. Combined with Wi-Fi, Bluetooth 5.3, and NFC for Google Wallet payments, you can stream music, navigate through Google Maps, handle phone calls, and send text messages directly from your wrist while leaving your smartphone behind.
All of this creates a solid Wear OS experience that brings Moto back to the frontier days of Android Wear. Moto was one of the original partners, and seeing it back is just fun. Even with the older chipset general performance shouldn’t leave any buyers wanting for much.
Wear OS smartwatches have historically struggled to cross the single-day battery threshold, but the Moto Watch Ultra promises up to two days of battery life under standard operating conditions from its 51-gram chassis.
In my 10 or so days in use, I’ve found the full 48 hours to be pretty accurate. I can get through two full days of notifications, walks, and around an hour-long workout, but that’s it. I’ve consistently woken up to the watch at less than 5% on each of my third mornings of use. 
When you do need to top off, the included magnetic USB-C charging dock supports fast charging. A quick 15-minute top-up yields roughly 24 hours of active use, making it easy to drop it on the charger while getting ready in the morning without giving up overnight sleep tracking. A full recharge takes about 45 minutes to an hour depending on how depleted it is.
Awarded to products with an average rating of 3.75 stars or higher, the AndroidGuys Smart Pick recognizes a balance of quality, performance, and value. 
Products with this distinction deserve to be on your short list of purchase candidates.
At $349.99, the Moto Watch Ultra (2026) makes a strong case for itself in a crowded wearable market. By combining Polar’s elite training metrics with full Wear OS capabilities, standalone LTE freedom, and a durable stainless-steel frame, Motorola has delivered a versatile smartwatch built for both daily productivity and serious athletic training.

  KETTLE  


        Pay no attention to those more practical risks behind the curtain, which is exactly what we're doing on this week's Kettle
    
Anthropic's IPO prospectus leaked this week, and boy was it filled with interesting details.
You can listen to the latest episode of The Kettle on this page, Spotify, Apple Podcasts, or YouTube. You can also subscribe on those platforms to be notified when a new episode goes live
For starters there's the fact a third of it is all about existential AI risks, then there's the fact that, despite some impressive revenue growth the company still has yet to turn a profit. Combine that with massive capex predictions, and you have an IPO with massive valuation, yet a long, hard road toward anything resembling earnings. 
Join host Brandon Vigliarolo, El Reg editor in chief Matt Rosoff, and enterprise IT reporter Lindsay Clark on this week's episode of The Kettle where they discuss the revelations, what they mean for the state of the AI industry, and just how much distraction from its actual problems Anthropic can eke out by keeping that doomsday narrative front and center. 
A lightly edited transcript is below: 
Brandon (00:04)
Hi everyone, and welcome to another episode of The Register's Kettle podcast. I'm your host, Brandon Vigliarolo, and this week we're gathered around the teapot to discuss hey, what else, AI? Specifically, the reported leak of Anthropic's IPO prospectus, which was apparently filled with all the doom, gloom, and to the moon promises for investors you'd expect from the world's most disruptive industry. With me to talk about the revelations are our editor-in-chief Matt Rosoff and enterprise IT reporter Lindsay Clark who brought us news of this whole mess. Thank you both for joining me.
Lindsay Clark (00:36)
Thank you.
Matt Rosoff (00:37)
Thanks for having us.
Brandon (00:38)
Yeah. So Lindsay, I know we didn't break this story. This was, I believe, a Financial Times exclusive, but what was reported about those IPO docs, in the story you wrote and just how apocalyptically optimistic is this whole thing?
Lindsay Clark (00:53)
Well we have this scenario where a company, Anthropic, is trying to sell itself by telling people that there's an existential risk to its technology, which I think is a bit of a weird way to go about it. But nonetheless, that's in the prospectus according to the Financial Times. And they also offer some insight into the numbers that are going around. Anthropic is still massively loss-making as far as we know.

60,2026-10-05,"Despite the rising popularity of electric vehicles (EVs) in recent years, many of these cars don't retain their value over time. In fact, according to iSeeCars, the overall EV segment sees a 57% drop in value over the first five years of ownership, a big loss for anyone who buys an EV and wants to resell it in the future. Some of this is due to the fast pace of change in automotive technology, making older cars less desirable. In addition, frequent price cuts on newer models and battery degradation concerns are contributing factors to this steep drop in an electric car's value. 
Sharp depreciation is one of the most common problems with electric vehicles, especially for those who plan to keep one for many years. After all, if a newer EV offers greater range, older models need to become cheaper to remain competitive. Some EVs are more sensitive to this value decrease than others, which makes the depreciation rate vary widely. However, well-known models like the Mustang Mach-E and Tesla Model Y are some of the most notable on this front. These, along with a few more key models, are electric vehicles that have seen more than a 50% value drop over five years.
When the Polestar 2 arrived in the U.S. in 2021, it was positioned as a luxury EV, but its resale value has plummeted since. The Launch Edition was originally priced at $61,200. Five years later, you can find this model for $20,400, which is a drop of 67%. And it wasn't just the Launch Edition that saw that level of depreciation. iSeeCars estimates the average Polestar 2 will depreciate 62% over five years. 
Those numbers put this Polestar EV among the models that lost the largest amount of value during the last five years. One of the reasons for that is how the Polestar 2 has evolved. The estimated range in 2021 was 233 miles. By 2024, the Long Range Single Motor version could reach up to 320 miles on a full charge, with faster charging speeds. That extra range and charging capability can make a difference when it comes to long-term value.
The Volkswagen ID.4 arrived in the United States in 2021 as one of the German automaker's early entries into the electric SUV market. The Pro version had a suggested price of $41,190, including destination, but that figure did not remain stable in the following years and dropped considerably. Today, you can find the same model for approximately $16,400, a loss of more than $24,000, despite its focus on offering owners a premium experience.
One factor that may have contributed to the ID.4 Pro's value decline of more than 50% in five years was the change in the incentives Volkswagen offered during that period. When it entered the market, VW included three years of free, unlimited DC fast charging through Electrify America. As of 2024, this perk was capped at 500 kwh. Today, only a few automakers offer free charging with their EVs, like the BMW i4 and some Mercedes-Benz EVs.
The Tesla Model S is one of the most well-known EVs in the U.S. market, but that hasn't stopped it from experiencing the same steep depreciation as the rest of the EV segment. The Plaid trimline was introduced in 2021, with a sticker of $141,190, while the Long Range was $91,100 at the time. Five years later, the KBB Fair Purchase Price for the Plaid is $51,600 and the Long Range is $38,900. This translates to a loss of 63%, against its original 2021 value, for the Tesla Model S Plaid. 
The Long Range saw a slightly smaller depreciation, but even so, owners who bought this EV new and held on to it have experienced a value loss of $52,000, or 57%. Tesla's price cuts over the years have played a part in the Model S losing value. In 2023, the Plaid's price was lowered to $114,990. That makes things harder for owners trying to sell older models, as used prices have to adjust to the lower cost of a new Model S. This is one key thing to keep in mind when buying a used Tesla.
Even though it was the first EV to be the world's best-selling car, the Tesla Model Y has failed to avoid a notable fall in resale values. The Model Y Performance started at $61,190 in 2021, while Long Range trimlines were $51,190. The same models now sell for an average of $29,100 and $28,100, respectively, in the U.S. That translates to a depreciation of 52% for the Model Y Performance after five years of ownership, compared to 45% for the Long Range variant. 
Tesla has repeatedly cut the prices of its own cars, including a $13,000 cut, nearly 20%, on the Model Y in 2023. Lower prices on new cars influence the second-hand market, bringing used car prices down as well. The arrival of a redesigned Model Y in 2025 added another layer of difficulty for used models to find new buyers, since they had a newer vehicle to consider, making it more difficult for earlier versions to maintain value.
With the Mustang Mach-E, Ford stepped into the EV segment in 2021 by combining the iconic Mustang name with a fully electric SUV. In the US, the model was released that year with a suggested price of $60,000 for the First Edition, and $61,600 for the GT model. The Mach-E later appeared among the most reliable electric SUVs, but that didn't stop it from depreciating significantly in the years that followed. 
In 2026, the Mustang Mach-E First Edition is worth $22,800, and you can expect to pay about $27,500 for the GT. That represents a 5-year depreciation of 62% for the First Edition and 55% for the Mach-E GT. Ford contributed to this, as it reduced the Mustang Mach-E's price on several occasions, including by $8,100 in 2024. New Mach-Es now offer up to 320 miles of range, compared to 300 miles for 2021 models, helping further push down prices on the used market. "
61,2026-10-05,"Ethereum's native token ether ETH$2,725.23 had a better third quarter than bitcoin BTC$85,602.28. Still, it got harder to trade, as liquidity, or how easily a token can be bought and sold without moving its price, thinned out.
Ether's price surged 70% in the quarter, outpacing bitcoin's 42% gain. Yet between July 6 and Sept. 30, its median daily market depth was just 35% to 45% of bitcoin's, according to a report by CoinGecko. In the same period last year, it was at least 60%. CoinGecko called it ""a stark drop from last year's figures.""
Market depth is the standard way to measure liquidity. It's the total dollar value of buy and sell orders sitting on exchanges within a set distance of the current price. The deeper the market, the more money it takes to move the price. In a thin market, a large order quickly eats through available orders and pushes the price further.
Ether had $13 million to $14 million in depth within 0.15% of its market price. In simple terms, that's roughly how much money was sitting in orders close enough to the price that clearing it would move ether by just 0.15%. Depth this close to the price matters most for everyday trades, and for large orders that traders want filled without moving the market.
The data undercuts a popular idea in markets that rising prices pull in more traders, and more traders mean deeper order books. That didn't happen with ether.
That said, ether is still fairly easy to trade. 
""ETH remains fairly liquid at this range [within 0.15% of the market price], with most exchanges maintaining over $1 million in depth on each side,"" CoinGecko said.
And ether isn't the only major token with thinner markets.
Liquidity in solana's SOL, ether's main rival, has also shrunk, though CoinGecko measured it over a wider range. ""The overall liquidity for SOL has shrunk considerably since 2025,"" the firm said.
SOL's depth within 2% of the market price fell from about $28 million on each side of the order book last year to around $20 million this year. Depth at 2% shows how much money sits in orders further from the current price. It's a gauge of how much selling or buying pressure the market can absorb before the price makes a bigger move, the kind seen during a sharp rally or sell-off. So while ether's thinning shows up right next to the price, SOL's shows up in its ability to handle larger swings.
XRP, the payments-focused cryptocurrency, held steady at around $30 million in total depth. But its order books leaned toward buyers during the study period, with close to $18 million in bids against $14 million in asks.
XRP's market cap is about 40% larger than SOL's, yet it has less depth within 2% of the price. That's because SOL still trades 25% more than XRP on an average day, CoinGecko said.


As stablecoins move into regulated finance, APAC is becoming a key proving ground. This report maps the region’s rules, use cases, and RLUSD’s role.
As stablecoins move into regulated finance, APAC is becoming a key proving ground. This report maps the region’s rules, use cases, and RLUSD’s role.
Why it matters:
As stablecoins move into regulated finance, APAC is becoming a key proving ground. This report maps the region’s rules, use cases, and RLUSD’s role.

BERITA BAHASA INDONESIA
TOK PISIN
Topic:Property Prices
The traditional spring selling boom is off to a poor start. (ABC News: Kate Ainsworth)
Property sales for the spring season are off to a weak start.
It comes off the back of two long weekends in major capital cities and the RBA lifting the cash rate again last week. 
Some economists predict capital city property prices could fall 10-15 per cent from peak to trough.
Australia's auction clearance rate has slumped to a three-month low, while new data shows the number of unsold homes has surged by more than 20 per cent in the past year.
Less than half the auctions that occurred across the country last week resulted in a successful sale.
The preliminary clearance rate in the past week fell to 48.2 per cent, its lowest since late June (47.4 per cent) and the second-lowest preliminary reading for the year so far.
It has also been hovering at or below the 50 per cent mark for several months, coinciding with a steep decline in property prices and weak sentiment in the housing market.
In the past week, 1,223 auctions were held in the nation's capital cities.
So there were 12.8 per cent fewer auctions than the previous week. It was also a significant 47.5 per cent drop from a year ago, according to new data from property research firm Cotality.
Less than half the homes that hit the market and went to auction sold in the last week, Cotality data shows.  (Supplied: Cotality)
Cotality's research director Tim Lawless said the weak result came off the back of long weekends in some states and the Reserve Bank's decision to lift interest rates for a fourth time this year.
It also comes after changes in this year's federal budget that sent shock waves through the property sector, including heavily restricted negative gearing, and in many cases higher capital gains tax for sellers.
Preliminary clearance rates across the major capitals last week compared with previous years.  (Supplied: Cotality)
Sydney and Melbourne ""have been a drag on the national result"", Mr Lawless said.
In Sydney, 304 auctions were held in the past week, down 61 per cent to the week before. It was also a 38.5 per cent drop compared to a year ago.
There were 670 auctions held in Melbourne. That was a 136 per cent jump compared to the previous week when the city was having the AFL grand final long weekend.
But in the past 12 months, the number of auctions held in Melbourne has dropped 45.6 per cent, the largest decline of any capital city.
Tim Lawless says clearance rates have hit a three-month low.  (ABC News: Jessica Ross)
Another real estate analysis firm, SQM Research, also released new data, showing some homes were sitting unsold on the market for as long as six months.
Homes that sat unsold for more than 180 days were clogging the market, with 10.5 per cent more older listings compared to a year ago.
Old housing stock is clogging the market.  (Supplied: SQM Research)
Spring's traditional selling boom has ground to a halt so far this season, with the number of unsold homes across the country totalling 276,000, a 21.6 per cent annual increase.
""The bulk of the increase is property that has been on the market for one to six months and hasn't sold,"" SQM Research director Louis Christopher said.
""In Sydney, fewer vendors are listing than a year ago, yet total stock is up almost a fifth. That's a demand problem, not a supply surge.""
The housing market is slumping and experts warn more falls are to come.  (ABC News: John Gunn)
Brisbane and Adelaide have had the largest increase in the number of properties available for sale, up 43.5 and 39.5 per cent respectively, according to SQM's data.
Melbourne, meanwhile, had 30.3 per cent more homes up for sale than it did a year ago.
Perth's figure was up 21.3 per cent, while Canberra's was up 17.8 per cent.
SQM's figures also showed there were 4,872 distressed sales across Australia in September, which was a 29 per cent jump compared to the same period last year.
It follows the ABC reporting last week that Australian housing values had dropped for a sixth straight month, with many economists warning there will be further price falls ahead.
Mr Lawless said Australian households were ""deeply pessimistic"" about the cost-of-living crisis, interest recent hikes and the budget's changes to property taxes creating ""less favourable conditions"".
""Homes are taking longer to sell, auction clearance rates have held below average and advertised stock levels have risen across many markets,"" he said.

                There's no evidence of distressed sales in the Gulf hotel market. But the war clearly changed the math buyers need to close deals.
            
The U.S.-Iran war hasn’t forced Gulf hotel owners to sell. But it has stalled deals as buyers and sellers can’t agree on price now that pre-war valuations no longer hold.
“A number of hotel assets that were being widely marketed prior to the recent disruption remain available for sale, reflecting a continuing mismatch between seller pricing expectations and buyer underwriting assumptions rather than immediate financial distress,"" said James Wrenn, partner and head of hotel and leisure at Knight Frank, in an email. 
""Motivated sellers are becoming increasingly pragmatic in their pricing expectations and recognising the need to account for recovery periods within short and medium-term forecasts, and therefore asking prices,"" he added. 
Investors are redoing their valuations, said Ali Siddiqui, research manager at consultancy Cavendish Maxwell.

Gulf hotel transactions have stalled since the U.S.-Iran war, not because owners are distressed but because buyers and sellers can't agree on price. Knight Frank's James Wrenn said hotels listed before the conflict remain unsold on price disagreement, with sellers becoming more pragmatic about the time assets will need to recover. Cavendish Maxwell's Ali Siddiqui described it as a wait-and-see stance, with investors redoing valuations built on pre-war demand and occupancy forecasts and watching Q4 for signs of recovery. The deal record is thin: five disclosed transactions worth about $1.45 billion between November 2024 and September 2026, roughly 80% of it in Dubai. The Gulf saw at least $883 million of hotel investment in 2025, led by Brookfield and Leela's $504 million purchase of Sofitel Dubai The Palm. In 2026 the total stands at $300 million from a single trade — AHS Properties' AED1.1 billion purchase of the Shangri-La Dubai, on terms agreed before the conflict. Capital is concentrating on income-producing assets in Riyadh, Makkah, Madinah, Dubai, Abu Dhabi, and Ras Al Khaimah, with HVS's Hala Matar Choufany saying pressure sits in highly leveraged hotels and those reliant on international arrivals, airline connectivity, and MICE. New supply has slowed in parallel: no hotels opened in Dubai or Abu Dhabi in Q2 2026, and the Gulf pipeline shrank by five projects and 1,103 rooms quarter over quarter."
64,2026-10-05,"The stock trading platform seeks to offer tokenized shares in more than 60 US-listed companies under the SEC’s recently introduced innovation exemption.
An OKX joint venture has filed with the US Securities and Exchange Commission to launch a tokenized stock trading platform.
OKXICE LLC, a joint venture between cryptocurrency exchange operator OKX and New York Stock Exchange parent Intercontinental Exchange Inc., has notified the SEC that it intends to launch a tokenized securities venue (TSV) under the SEC’s new innovation exemption.
The platform will include more than 60 companies listed on US stock exchanges, said OKXICE co-chair Andrew Cuomo.
“The digital asset revolution is already transforming our financial system. Tokenized securities are part of what comes next,” he added. 
A public notice dated Oct. 4 lists 63 proposed stock symbols, including Nvidia, Apple, Microsoft and Tesla, alongside crypto companies such as Strategy, Coinbase, Circle and Bitgo. 
The OKXICE platform plans to operate 24 hours a day, seven days a week, using permissioned Uniswap v4 liquidity pools deployed on XLayer. Each tokenized stock would be paired with USDC (USDC), Global Dollar (USDG) or USDt (USDT). 
OKX and Intercontinental Exchange formed a 50-50 joint venture in June to build infrastructure for tokenized financial products.
In September, the SEC issued a temporary exemption allowing limited trading of tokenized US stocks on certain onchain venues. Under the innovation exemption, TSVs can offer permissioned trading of tokenized National Market System (NMS) stocks through automated market makers and liquidity pools.
Related: Winners and losers of the SEC’s new tokenized stock rules

65,2026-10-05,"This is Naked Capitalism fundraising week. 1302 donors have already invested in our efforts to combat corruption and predatory conduct, particularly in the financial realm.
