Having cleared the biggest legal hurdle, the Ellison media empire has assumed control of WBD, dumped all over the bond markets, and named its leadership team for the new organization which will be called Skydance after David Ellison’s studio.
Let’s start with legal case closing.
Cases Closed, WBD Goes to the Ellisons
Forbes sums it up:
Judge Araceli Martinez-Olguin ruled Wednesday to approve the agreement, which was reached as part of the states’ antitrust lawsuit seeking to block Paramount and Warner Bros.’ merger.
Her ruling means the settlement agreement can take effect and Paramount and Warner Bros. can close their deal, as the merger had already received government approval from the U.S. and other countries.
Martinez-Olguin ruled the settlement “represents a reasonable factual and legal resolution of the dispute,” is “procedurally sound” and was reached fairly between the parties.
The settlement agreement requires Paramount to produce 30 to 32 films for the next five years, a certain share of which have to be produced in the U.S., and imposes other requirements like mandating Paramount and Warner Bros. negotiate their channels’ cable packages separately and establishing an “independent editorial board” to oversee CNN and CBS News.
David Sirota had a beautiful riff on The Left Hook about the cynicism of California Governor Gavin Newsom successfully pressuring California AG Rob Bonta into essentially conceding the case against Paramount while positioning himself as the leading anti-Trump Democrat:
David Sirota: …the Democratic attorneys general, 12 of them, had the chance to really prevent a kind of total takeover of the media by Donald Trump’s allies, billionaires, right? The true oligarchy of the oligarchy.
And what ended up happening instead was Gavin Newsom, who had originally appointed Rob Bonta AG, Gavin Newsom came out very hard and said, “I don’t think the AGs should block this.”
And Rob Bonta reversed himself and did a settlement. A settlement that included (this is an important point, it’s a legal point) no structural remedies.
And when you hear that term, what that means is the settlement has all of these alleged pledges of behavioral changes, like, you know, the studios will do this, they’ll set up an independent journalism board. I call them the inshallah pledges. Right. It’s like, these will behave nicely, right? Right.
Structural remedies, which were not in the settlement, are: we’re going to sell off this, we’re going to divest that, so that we don’t control everything. A lot of times, antitrust settlements include structural settlements so that what is being created in the wake of a merger isn’t a monopoly. There were no structural remedies in this.
So pull back a second, and what you see is that the Democratic leaders of one of the most Democratic states in the country handed over a giant segment of the already consolidated information ecosystem that we live in, the media ecosystem that we live in. They handed it over to Donald Trump’s oligarchs.
So what’s amazing to me, I mean, the sociopathy, Gavin Newsom here is actually, I struggle to find words for it, because this is a person who is concurrently going out into public saying, “I am Mr. Anti-Trump. I am the face of the resistance.” And the face of the resistance simultaneously handed over a media empire, effectively.
And now on to the bond market reaction.
Many Billions in Paramount Debt Hits the Market Hard, Mr Market Cries Out
The Wrap reported on Paramount’s debt offering on September 28:
The debt offering, which was filed with the SEC on Monday, includes $32 billion in investment-grade debt and $12.4 billion in high yield bonds. The proceeds from the debt offering will be combined with equity and term loan financing as well as cash on hand to fund the $110 billion purchase of Warner Bros. Discovery.
By October 1, Bloomberg was reporting on the market reaction:
Just hours after Paramount Skydance Corp. issued $52 billion of debt to fund the biggest Hollywood buyout ever, investors were nursing more than $100 million of losses, triggering a flurry of angry calls from money managers to Wall Street banks that underwrote the debt.
The company’s junk bonds were among the hardest hit in initial trading, with the eight-year US dollar notes changing hands at about 96 cents on the dollar on Thursday after selling for 100 cents on Wednesday. The loans and high-grade bonds broadly weakened as well, and the cost of betting against the company’s credit surged to a 17-year high. The corporate bond market was generally softer.
When trading opened on Thursday morning, the bonds and loans immediately fell, although later in the session, much of the debt had taken back some or even all of the losses. It was an unusual turn on Wall Street, where underwriters try to sell debt at levels that will allow modest, quick gains, to encourage investors to keep buying. The blockbuster debt sale followed by immediate losses recalled a similar debacle in June, when SpaceX sold $25 billion of bonds that soon performed poorly.
In the case of Paramount, some investors were in the rare position of getting most or all the debt they asked for as others balked at the last minute, especially for long-dated portions. That left money managers unexpectedly overloaded — and looking to quickly reduce their exposure, according to people with knowledge of the transaction.
Paramount lost more than half of investor orders for its longest-dated investment-grade bonds
As the selling pressure caused debt prices to plummet in the early morning hours in New York, traders expressed their frustration to underwriters Bank of America and Citigroup through messages and phone calls, said the people, who asked not to be named because the discussions were private.
Many focused their outrage on the high-grade order book being touted as topping $109 billion, only for a significant amount of that demand to vanish as the banks sharply lowered premiums offered on the debt, according to messages seen by Bloomberg. Others pinned blame primarily on the junk-rated portion, which then dragged the investment-grade bonds lower.
As of Monday October 5, this is how Paramount’s bonds are looking:

Financial Time’s Alphaville puzzled about the bond ratings, pointed out that Larry Ellison’s Oracle holdings were backing the debt and did some analysis:
We checked in on how investors are pricing the global AI hyperscaler’s creditworthiness. We also threw the new Paramount Skydance bonds, and every other triple-B-rated US corporate bond in the ICE BofA corporate bond index into the chart.

Increasingly, it looks like two large issuers are being priced as having far higher risk credit premia than the rest. Both sit on the lowest rung of the investment-grade ladder, and both are Ellison family companies.
Moreover, as Chris Whittall of IFR noted today, the cost of insuring Paramount debt against default using credit default swaps has skyrocketed. And that Ellison family commitment doesn’t look like it has helped (h/t to Dec Mullarkey, who also flagged this correlation):

Who’s Backing This Play?
Skydance’s roughly $80 billion of debt is now spread across thousands of bond and loan investors. Apollo Global, which committed a $54 billion bridge loan in December, might be happy they never had to fund that loan and appears to have only served as an underwriter and agent of the latest round of bond sales and as of now, we don’t know how much of the final debt it, or any other private credit fund, kept.
But we do know a little bit about who has the controlling stakes in the Ellison Media Empire — remember Larry Ellison’s stake in American TikTok (TikTok USDS) is separate from Skydance.
The Ellison family holds 77.5% of the voting Class A stock and Gerry Cardinale’s RedBird Capital holds the other 22.5%. Most of the money comes from Larry Ellison’s family trust, which committed up to $46.72 billion of new equity. He had personally backstopped the bid with a $40 billion guarantee in December. RedBird’s Fund IV put in just $250 million for the Warner deal, on top of the roughly $2 billion it invested in the 2025 Skydance-Paramount merger.
Cardinale stays on the board, and his former RedBird partner Andy Gordon is president of Skydance.
About $24 billion of the Ellison commitment was passed on to three Gulf sovereign wealth funds. Saudi Arabia’s PIF gets 15.1% of the company, Abu Dhabi’s L’imad 12.8% and the Qatar Investment Authority 10.6%. That is 38.5% in all, held as non-voting shares, and each fund is cleared to go to 20%.
The Gulf funds gave up board seats and other governance rights to keep the deal outside a national-security review. They provide the money and get no vote.
The Ellison trust and RedBird remain liable for the full commitment even after selling pieces of it on. That ties the deal to Larry Ellison’s own balance sheet, which depends heavily on Oracle stock. In September, he filed to sell up to $7.5 billion of Oracle shares, then cancelled the sale a day later. Bloomberg reports that investors treat Paramount and Oracle as one Larry Ellison credit risk.
Now, let’s learn about the new Skydance leadership team.
Bari Weiss Tops Out, Ynon Kreiz to Run the Show
Much to the relief of many critics who have been alarmed at Bari Weiss’ actions at CBS News (and their plummeting ratings), she won’t be taking over CNN. Instead, Mark Thompson will stay on as  Chairman and Editor-in-Chief of CNN Worldwide, and both will report directly to David Ellison and co-CEO Ynon Kreiz.
Rumors continue to swirl that Weiss is doomed via the NY Post and Radar Online.
Let’s learn more about Ynon Kreiz.
Barbie’s Old Boss Co-Taking Over The Ellison Media Empire
Puck titled their piece on Kreiz’ hiring “Ynon Sequitur“:
On Wednesday, minutes after a judge approved the consent decree for his long-gestating merger, David Ellison announced that he had tapped Ynon Kreiz to serve as co-C.E.O. of the combined Paramount–Warner Bros. Discovery—an advanced iteration of the old Jeff Shell role, this time with real authority and direct reports. Ynon, a veteran Israeli-American media executive and fixture among the Polo Lounge crowd, has spent the past eight years as C.E.O. of toy-making giant Mattel, and his transfer set off a chain reaction of executive moves: Condé Nast C.E.O. Roger Lynch will take the Mattel job, leaving the Newhouses to identify a new leader for that shrinking business.
Ynon had a mixed tenure at Mattel: He leveraged its I.P. for film and TV deals with varying success—Warner’s Barbie was a hit, Masters of the Universe flopped—but never made good on a promise to diversify into higher-margin businesses, like games. Mattel’s sales have been flat or declining for years, suggesting a failure to capitalize on Barbie’s success. Most importantly, the stock largely traded downward during his tenure despite relentless belt-tightening, underperforming a 200 percent gain in the S&P. “He was great at cutting costs—he cut costs to the bone,” one Mattel veteran told me. “But there was zero innovation.”
…David has been disciplined about surrounding himself with seasoned C.E.O.s. One of the running myths in the creative community is that David’s youth and inexperience will wreak havoc on these storied studios, resulting in 30 Flyboys a year. In reality, he has so far been diligent about delegating management of the business to long-tenured professionals, and his decision to appoint a co-C.E.O. rather than a more amorphous chief strategy officer or president—Shell’s own title—evidences a whiff of sangfroid.
…David has been formally assembling a team and providing some much-needed clarity to the org chart. Surprising no one, HBO C.E.O. Casey Bloys will run the combined entity’s streaming business, which is why Paramount streaming chief Cindy Holland vacated the building earlier this week. “They are both great executives, but you really can’t let Casey walk out the door,” one veteran media executive said. Of course, David Zaslav had the same philosophy when he inherited Bloys in the Discovery–WarnerMedia merger. Indeed, Casey’s untouchable status in Hollywood is one of the great, underexamined subplots of the Warner story.
One major player who’s already lost the inevitable game of executive musical chairs necessary to execute a merger like this is the head of TNT Sports, per CNBC:
Luis Silberwasser, chairman and CEO of TNT Sports, is departing the soon-to-be merged Paramount Skydance and Warner Bros. Discovery, according to people familiar with the matter.
David Berson, president and chief executive officer of CBS Sports, will lead a newly formed global sports group that will oversee all networks, platforms and territories. The CBS Sports, TNT Sports and WBD Sports brands will roll up into the new group.
There are other major players at WBD who seem unlikely to last into the Skydance era. Puck’s Matt Belloni reports that Mike de Luca and Pam Abdy, who have run Warner’s Film Division for years, are among that number.
Belloni also roasts departing WBD boss David Zaslav:
David Zaslav threw himself a farewell party last night, because of course he did.
…
Zaslav’s message was along the lines of, Look what we accomplished, of course, but as of next week, the century-old Warner Bros. studio will cease to exist. Zaslav loaded it up with debt and false promises, then merged it with longtime rival Paramount in a miraculous $110 billion deal that will allow him to abscond with up to $800 million in plunder—sorry, bonuses and stock grants he negotiated at arm’s length with the totally independent compensation committee of his board.
Belloni then talks about the Warner film division and the Mike and Pam years:
…guessing the fate of De Luca and Abdy at the Warner Bros. film unit has been a parlor game around town, especially as they unleashed the extremely poorly timed Digger. Until now, at least. Mike and Pam will not be joining the merged company, per two well-placed sources.
…
the Mike and Pam strategy since taking over in 2022. Admired by filmmakers (and the agents for whom the phrase “full freight” is erotica), the duo’s outsize bets on auteurs worked spectacularly with Ryan Coogler’s Sinners but failed with Maggie Gyllenhaal’s The Bride and, at least financially, with P.T.A.’s One Battle After Another, among others. Cruise, when thanking De Luca and Abdy at the Digger L.A. premiere on Monday, proclaimed, “You didn’t even take a breath when we brought you this film. I don’t even think you turned the page on it. You’re just like—you’re in.” I thought about that comment a lot while watching the movie.
With that strategy, the 2026 Warners slate has yet to deliver a single $100 million domestic grosser, and there are flashing danger signs into next year, with troubled or delayed or pricey risks from Jonah Hill, J.J. Abrams, Sam Esmail, and Nancy Meyers, among others. Ellison wants to make G.I. Joe with Bradley Cooper, as he announced today. He wants to make a Cruise movie—but Days of Thunder 2, not Digger. Mike and Pam made some stuff like that, too, but not enough, and their brand, since back at MGM and even before that, is the big swing. They love it, they love that filmmakers love it, and they know that you’ve got to take risks to surprise audiences. As a movie fan, I also love that proposition. But it’s not my money. So now they’ll be paid out through 2030, and let the rumors commence about potential jobs at Netflix or Sony or wherever.
Instead, Ellison is elevating Josh Greenstein and Dana Goldberg, who currently run Paramount, to oversee both studios and those promised 30 releases a year.
Greenstein and Goldberg have been the subject of snark around town regarding their supposedly iffy taste and filmmaker relationships. But Ellison trusts them, and since taking over Paramount last summer, they’ve ramped up production and development, including launching a low-budget label and pursuing sequels to Star Trek, Paranormal Activity, and World War Z, plus a Call of Duty movie. On his most recent earnings call, Ellison boasted that the studio division generated more than $200 million of adjusted EBITDA in the first half of ’26. That includes more than just the film unit, but the better numbers were helped by Scream 7, Scary Movie, and “improved slate profitability,” C.F.O. Dennis Cinelli told shareholders. Lots of singles and doubles.
For better and worse, the Greenstein/Goldberg brand is decidedly not big artistic swings at high costs.
The Paramount-WBD merger combined with his many recent moves at Oracle has increased media scrutiny of Ellison’s father Larry, let’s check in on that.
Calling Vanity Fair
Any titan of industry who’s been getting bad press knows to tell their PR team to contact Vanity Fair for one of those patented celebrity profiles, and Larry’s no exception. The piece, titled “Inside Larry Ellison’s Wide Web of Power, Money and Politics” hit on October 1st:
VF’s Tom Dotan notes Ellison’s identification with the octopus and decribes two of his tentacles as Oracle and Skydance before discussing Ellison’s political portfolio:
A third appendage winds toward political power. As Silicon Valley staged its noisy migration to Trumpism, Ellison worked quietly to influence the president, establishing an effective if not always close relationship. Outside America, he bankrolls his friend Tony Blair’s nonprofit organization, which pushes governments to adopt new technologies like AI. And he has been a steadfast supporter of Benjamin Netanyahu, hosting the prime minister on Lanai while the Israeli leader faced corruption charges at home.
I’ve argued previously that Ellison’s closeness to Trump is much overstated and that he and David had to rely on Trump’s former agent Ari Emanuel to gain Trump’s support for Skydance’s 2025 acquisition of Paramount. Note also that Ellison was not included in the recent White House meeting where top tech executives signed “an accord” on AI safety.
The VF piece also delves into Ellison’s decision to massively increase Oracle’s capex spending — and with it corporate debt:
…unlike Amazon, Google, and the other cloud giants that are otherwise ­money machines, Uncle Larry did not have the cash. Instead Oracle turned to debt, and in a terrifying way. In its last fiscal year, the company raised $43 billion through bond offerings, pushing total borrowings toward $130 billion. Capital expenditures more than doubled, and its long-healthy free cash flow cratered to -$23.7 billion.
The deeper the red on the balance sheet, the more uneasy the bondholders grew. In July, S&P cut the company’s credit rating to a notch above junk. Lenders started to balk at some Oracle projects; the company backed out of a planned expansion of the Abilene campus. In September, it sent a force majeure notice on its New Mexico site, seeking to defer payments if the project slips, though Oracle insists it’s on schedule. Some loans tied to the project now trade around 90 cents on the dollar, the Financial Times reported.
Mounting worries caused the company’s stock to give back all its gains—and then some. Since peak, Oracle shares have fallen by more than half; Ellison’s own net worth has dropped some $200 billion, from a high of $400 billion to about $217 billion in September.
“Ellison has really put it all on red,” says John Hempton of Bronte Capital, a global hedge fund. “I can’t tell if he did it because he’s a gambling man or out of weakness.”
Many leaders in the tech industry have looked at how much the company has borrowed with an amused sense of awe. “Imagine if you put in all that capex and it all fizzles out?” wondered one former Oracle executive. But the optimistic case is simpler than it looks: It would take OpenAI going to zero for Oracle’s data center bet to fully blow up. And Oracle is already touting returns, boasting that OpenAI’s latest model, Astra, was trained in Abilene.
“Go back to last year and everyone thought Sam was certifiable with how much capacity it would take to run AI,” says Stifel analyst Reback. “But at this point, to some extent, OpenAI is too big to fail.”
Too big to fail, if that isn’t living the dream, I don’t know what is.

66,2026-10-05,"The artificial intelligence boom may be changing more than just stock prices.
AI could alter where interest rates ultimately settle by boosting demand, lifting productivity, and increasing capital investment, Bank of Japan Deputy Governor Shinichi Uchida said Monday.
""First, it is a big positive demand shock, which has put upward pressure on the economy and prices. Second, it could affect the supply side, perhaps positively by raising productivity and enhancing capital stock accumulations,"" Uchida said in a speech in Tokyo.
He added that the developments could affect r-star, or the neutral rate of interest, which is the level that neither stimulates nor restrains the economy.
If AI raises that underlying rate, it could mean central banks ultimately need to keep policy rates higher than investors previously expected to keep inflation in check without unnecessarily slowing economic activity.
AI has been a ""favorite topic"" among central bankers in recent years, Uchida said, but what was once largely a conceptual discussion about the future has become an immediate issue for monetary policy.
His remarks come as investors debate whether the recent surge in long-term bond yields reflects a temporary repricing or a more lasting shift in the level where interest rates eventually settle. Uchida suggested AI may be another reason investors and policymakers need to rethink that destination.
That question carries particular weight in Japan, which spent much of the past three decades battling deflation and ultra-low interest rates before the BOJ began tightening policy.
On Monday, the benchmark 10-year JGB yield stood at 3.09% around midday in Japan, hovering near a three-decade high. The 30-year yield touched a record 4.235%.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

If the neutral rate is rising, higher borrowing costs may not just be part of the BOJ's exit from ultra-loose policy — they may be here to stay.
Uchida's speech adds to a growing debate over how AI could reshape global capital flows.
Rising Japanese government bond yields are narrowing the yield advantage that encouraged domestic investors to buy overseas bonds for years, potentially reducing demand for US Treasurys.
Last week, strategists at ING said AI could account for roughly one-fifth of the recent rise in long-term bond yields, citing heavy borrowing to finance data centers and other infrastructure, as well as expectations that the technology will lift productivity and long-run economic growth.

Gov. Kathy Hochul was one of a handful of Democratic governors to stockpile pills used in medication abortion amid fears the U.S. Supreme Court could restrict access three years ago.
But a feared shortage of the drug never materialized. Now, 150,000 pills in the state’s possession have all expired — and not a single one was distributed.
The state Department of Health says it will soon destroy its reserve of misoprostol pills, which is enough for 12,500 doses. That’s in contrast to California, which took steps to distribute its supply to providers, and Oregon, which reupped its stock at no cost.
Some legal threats to medication abortion remain. But with misoprostol still readily available, New York has no current plans to restock its supply, according to the Department of Health.
“Despite ongoing politically motivated attacks on reproductive healthcare across the nation, New York remains a safe harbor for anyone seeking these critical services,” said Cadence Acquaviva, a spokesperson for the agency.
Hochul’s decision to amass a major reserve of misoprostol in 2023 came after a federal judge’s ruling in Texas called into question whether mifepristone, a separate drug used in medication abortion, would be taken off the market.
The state Department of Health would go on to acquire the 150,000 misoprostol pills at a cost of $112,000, according to the agency. But when separate court rulings kept mifepristone on the shelves, the state never tapped into its stockpile.
Acquaviva said the stockpile was meant to ensure New Yorkers “could access this safe and legal reproductive health medicine” amid legal uncertainty.
Misoprostol and mifepristone are the two major drugs used in medication abortion, which accounted for 63% of all abortions in the national healthcare system in 2023, according to the Guttmacher Institute.
Often, the two drugs are prescribed together, with a patient taking mifepristone first and following up with misoprostol. But doctors can also prescribe a course that solely consists of 12 misoprostol pills to end an early-term pregnancy.
Washington, Massachusetts, Oregon and California were among the other Democratic-led states to stock up on misoprostol in the wake of conflicting court rulings on mifepristone in 2023.
Since then, the states have taken different approaches to their expiring supplies.
Gov. Hochul spoke about abortion rights at a 2022 press conference.
In California, health officials took steps to distribute more than 12,000 misoprostol doses to healthcare providers across the state about a year before they expired, according to LAist.
Washington state took steps to destroy its $1.3 million stockpile last year, while Oregon state officials were able to swap 22,500 doses for a new batch that expires in 2028 at no cost, according to the Oregon Capital Chronicle.
In 2023, Hochul announced plans to purchase 150,000 doses of misoprostol — or approximately 1.8 million pills, enough for a five-year supply. The Department of Health started with a smaller purchase, buying 12,500 doses from drug wholesaler AmerisourceBergen, now known as Cencora. The agency declined to purchase more after the feared shortage never materialized.
New York’s pills are currently being held in a secure location, according to the health department. They’ll be destroyed the next time the agency issues a contract to dispose of expired items in its Medical Emergency Response Cache.
Federal courts, meanwhile, continue to consider the availability of mifepristone.
In 2024, the Supreme Court dismissed the case at the center of the Texas judge’s decision that led the Democratic governors to build up a misoprostol stockpile, though the court’s ruling was on technical grounds.
In May of this year, a separate Supreme Court order kept mifepristone available via telehealth appointments while a lawsuit works its way through the lower courts.
On Wednesday, three states — Louisiana, Alabama and Arkansas — sued New York and two other states in federal court over their shield laws, which protects providers who prescribe abortion pills and mail them to patients in other states.
“As anti-abortion extremists in other states try to reach across our borders and threaten patients and providers, New York will stand up for them,” Hochul said in a statement.
David Lombardo is host of WCNY’s “The Capitol Pressroom,” a member of the New York Public News Network.

68,2026-10-05,"Like all e-waste, the deluge of aging solar panels is concerning as they reach their end-of-life. By 2030, we're expected to see over 8.8 million tons of waste from photovoltaic panels (PVs). But there's one panel technology, in particular, that carries some fairly nasty and dangerous materials. Called cadmium telluride thin film photovoltaics, or CdTe, they're relatively low-cost to manufacture, effective, and make up over 30% of the U.S. PV market. They have a lifespan of 20 to 30 years, but some of the oldest were installed in the early 2000's. That means we're coming up on their expiration date soon, and they cannot be tossed away like common trash.
If these panels end up in landfills, moisture can carry toxins from the cadmium and tellurium — referred to as landfill leachate — and enter the groundwater, exposing the area to potential toxins. The chemicals are distributed inside a panel as a thin, solid semiconductor layer, but something like rainwater coming into contact with it can build up and leach the toxins. Of course, that's less likely to happen if the panels remain intact, unbroken, and sealed, or if the facility has proper leachate mitigation, but there's no guarantee of that when tossed into a disorderly landfill. 
It's not the panels themselves that are the problem, but rather the materials inside. Solar farms of all kinds are having a hugely positive effect on local wildlife, and the broader environment, when they're in use. But since a whopping 90% of expired or defective panels end up in landfills after they've run their course — it costs less to dump than recycle — the concerns are warranted.
There are teams of researchers that have developed ways to properly recycle and dispose of the materials inside the CdTe panels. By using electrochemical solutions the cadmium and tellurium can be extracted. The big problem is that these methods require specialized solutions, and the infrastructure for building and sustaining facilities on a large scale isn't quite there yet. Then there's the matter of education. While it's easier and less costly to throw the resulting panels in a landfill, that's absolutely not what should be happening. Everyone needs to understand how to properly and safely deal with old panels.
The EPA recommends that homeowners contact state and local recycling agencies for guidance on how to dispose of them. There are some smaller recyclers that can take in CdTe panels, and some companies, like First Solar, develop their technologies from the get-go so they're suitable for recycling. Around 90% of the materials in First Solar's modules can be recovered and reused after they reach end-of-life.
With proper care and maintenance, the expected lifecycle of the average solar panel is formidable. It takes around 30 years for them to lower to 80% efficiency, which means, you may not even need to replace them completely by that time. Many panels being purchased, installed, and put into use today have a distant expiration. But if you do have old panels, understanding how and where your local regulations apply is important. You can also find creative ways to repurpose old solar equipment, but you'll still want to be mindful of the hazards.
The EPA recognizes cadmium is a toxic substance, and considers it to be carcinogenic. Tellurium has been known to cause damage to organs in both rats and humans. Therefore, it is ill advised to expose yourself to potential toxins, which means staying away from broken, shattered, or damaged PVs. Exposure to cracked cells may even warrant cancer screenings.
If you're near solar panels or working around them, it's a smart idea to wear the proper protective equipment, including gloves, coverings, and even respirators. The Occupational Safety and Health Administration (OSHA) recommends wearing eye and face coverings and physical protective equipment around solar hardware. OSHA also recommends periodic inspections and tests of solar panel facilities. 
This is all going to be very different for residential folks, and if you're a homeowner working on or with your own solar setup. It's likely to be just you with no one else measuring or testing before you get to work. But it's important to be aware that you could be working with dangerous and toxic materials so that you can take the necessary precautions."

70,2026-10-05,"The Commodity Futures Trading Commission is proposing a pair of rules on Monday to establish U.S. cryptocurrency oversight under its powers to regulate leveraged and margin-dependent trades, trying to fill in regulatory uncertainties left by the inability of Congress to finish the job of passing a crypto law.
The U.S. derivatives agency is pursuing two regulatory pathways it intends as a ""comprehensive regulatory framework,"" according to agency officials — one that directly handles transactions and another that governs the firms hosting the activity. The latter will establish a new category of platforms known as crypto asset markets (CAMs).
Crypto activity associated with leverage, margin or financing would fall into the CFTC's regulatory world under the rules. So, if traders want to use borrowed funds to amplify their positions, that would fall into this oversight.
""Today, the CFTC is doing its part to deliver clear rules of the road for crypto asset markets with its advanced notice of proposed rulemaking on Regulation Crypto Asset Transactions (Regulation CTX) and Regulation Crypto Asset Markets (Regulation CAM),"" CFTC Chairman Mike Selig said in remarks prepared for delivery at Fordham Law's annual Blockchain Regulatory Symposium. ""These rules would codify a pathway for crypto asset exchanges to operate under uniform national oversight by the CFTC pursuant to the same statutory authorities that the prior administration instead utilized to regulate by enforcement.""
But the effort may continue to leave a significant gap because of the regulator's missing authority to oversee spot markets — the direct trading of crypto in which the assets change hands in their original form at current market prices, without leverage or margin considerations. That would include the unvarnished buying and selling of the largest swath of crypto tokens, such as bitcoin BTC$85,602.28 and Ethereum's ether ETH$2,704.24, with one major exception: The CFTC can still police fraud and manipulation in those markets.
The agency's new efforts otherwise can't touch or replace the states' money-transmission regulations as the governing rules of direct trading, though CFTC officials said that firms that want to offer more complex products would do so through tailored, CFTC-regulated platforms. The officials said they're not yet sure what the scale of the remaining spot market will be until they hear more from the industry in the 60-day public comment period these proposals are opening up, though they suggested that consumers may prefer to do business in the federally regulated space.
Many of the major platforms are already registered as designated contract markets (DCMs), such as Coinbase, Crypto.com and Bitnomial, and also prediction markets Kalshi and Polymarket. The new crypto subcategory would be narrower than that full DCM status, but if firms want to pursue futures, swaps and options, they'll need the DCM stamp.

Closing the spot-market gap was at the core of the Digital Asset Market Clarity Act that stalled in the U.S. Senate last month. Since that legislative setback, the CFTC — alongside its larger, sister agency, the Securities and Exchange Commission — has been moving forward on crypto policies to make up for the absence of a new U.S. market structure law.
The SEC had moved well ahead of the CFTC in proposing rules — including one late last week on how investment firms should maintain custody of crypto assets — and implementing an exemption that clears the way for securities tokenization. With Monday's actions, the CFTC is catching up, and officials suggested that more will come later, because Chairman Selig wants to further cement some of the earlier staff guidance on crypto matters.

Both agencies are currently led by only Republican commissioners as President Donald Trump still hasn't offered any nominees to fill each five-member commission. At the SEC, that's now just Chairman Paul Atkins and Commissioner Mark Uyeda. At the CFTC, Chairman Mike Selig has been the sole commissioner for nearly a year, meaning he has been taking unilateral actions akin to agencies established with a single director.

Earlier this year, the two agencies worked together to issue what they referred to as a token taxonomy that, for the first time, tried to clearly define how they would characterize the assets that would fall either under the SEC's or the CFTC's authority. The SEC had been working on rules governing whatever lands in its jurisdiction, and the CFTC is now joining to outline the regulations on its side of the divide.

The CFTC's proposed rules are set within the Commodity Exchange Act's retail-trading elements established under the 2010 Dodd-Frank Act. That financial-system overhaul was meant to further protect consumers after the economic meltdown of 2008.

The new CAM regulatory status is meant as an option that exchanges can pursue, akin to the tailored types of charters available in the regulated banking sector. But they'll be held to the kind of standards already in place for other registrants, including a prohibition against listing products that could be vulnerable to manipulation and “proof-of-reserves” demands for exchanges that keep customer assets in omnibus accounts.
Crypto trading activity under the Regulation CTX definitions would also require futures commission merchants (FCMs) act as intermediaries, according to the agency, ensuring the involvement of Bank Secrecy Act money-laundering safeguards.
The agency's ""actual delivery"" exemption will also allow for transactions to be exempt that involve the real exchange of assets in less than 28 days.

Selig additionally said in his speech that his agency is looking into policies meant to protect software developers who are creating products but aren't themselves soliciting or taking orders or holding customers' funds. ""A person should not have to register as an introducing broker simply because that person shipped code,"" he said.

As stablecoins move into regulated finance, APAC is becoming a key proving ground. This report maps the region’s rules, use cases, and RLUSD’s role.
As stablecoins move into regulated finance, APAC is becoming a key proving ground. This report maps the region’s rules, use cases, and RLUSD’s role.

71,2026-10-05,"RAM prices have shot up so much in the last year that a 32GB DDR5 kit can cost more than a flagship gaming CPU like the 9800X3D. That's a tough pill to swallow if you're planning to build a new PC today, but it's even worse if you're upgrading from an older AM4 build. And those who remember these kits being available for just $100 early last year will probably have an even harder time convincing themselves to pay four or five times as much. It's why many gamers I've talked to are totally fine sticking with AM4 until prices come down.
I wanted to take the same route when I saw RAM prices creeping up, but decided to bite the bullet and splurge on a 32GB DDR5-6000 kit back in December because I figured prices would get completely out of hand with the massive demand from AI data centers. Looking back, that was the right call since the same kit costs about $150–200 more than it did back then. But even if I were on the fence about upgrading to AM5 today, I'd still probably do the same thing because nobody knows if or when prices will come down.
Is there a light at the end of this tunnel? 
I'm not just speculating here when I say supply is only going to get tighter. That's coming from Micron directly. The company may not sell consumer RAM anymore under its Crucial brand, but it's still one of the biggest DRAM manufacturers in the world, so it has a pretty good idea of where the market is headed. During its Q4 earnings call on October 1, Micron said it expects supply-demand conditions to be much tighter in 2027 and 2028 than they've been this year.
Sure, you could argue there's a chance this AI bubble deflates by then and data center demand eases. Even if that happens, Micron expects huge demand from humanoid robots, with each unit requiring as much memory as Level 4 autonomous vehicles, which already use over 200GB. So, if you're thinking you'll hold onto AM4 with that 5800X3D until RAM prices come back down, you're going to need a lot of patience. At this rate, we could be talking 2029 before things finally start looking better.
Earlier this year, AMD announced it would extend AM5 support through 2029. Mind you, this is the second time AMD has extended support, after promising support through 2027 at Computex 2024. So I really doubt the company will extend it again. And now that you know RAMpocalypse could drag on for a couple more years, 2029 would probably be the worst year to switch to AM5. You'd essentially be buying into the platform right when AMD's extended support commitment ends.
Remember, a big part of AM5's appeal is its platform longevity. In fact, you've already experienced it with AM4 if you're still holding onto a 5800X3D today. The platform lasted long enough for you to upgrade your CPU several years later without replacing your motherboard or RAM, and AM5 gives you the same opportunity right now, even if it's halfway through its lifecycle. But if you wait until 2029 to make that switch, you're unlikely to get any drop-in upgrades later on unless you get lucky and AMD surprises us with another CPU as it did with AM4 and the 5800X3D in 2022.
When a 32GB DDR5-6000 kit costs more than a 9800X3D, I can't blame anyone for refusing to bite the bullet right now. You're already paying hundreds of dollars for a new CPU and motherboard to move to AM5, and for the longest time, RAM prices were the easiest to overlook when 32GB kits used to cost $100. But today, that's the most expensive part of your upgrade, so if you want to hold onto your 5800X3D for dear life, be my guest.
But that doesn't mean you should get the 5800X3D Anniversary Edition today if you're still on an older AM4 CPU. At $349, you're spending even more money on a platform you already know has nowhere left to go. And for what? To wait another two or three years hoping DDR5 prices finally come back down? I'd rather spend that money on a platform with a few more years left and get it over with. A drop-in CPU swap is one of the last cheap PC upgrades left these days, and if prices don't come back down by 2028 or 2029, at least you can get one of the fastest gaming CPUs on the market for a few hundred dollars.
There's really no perfect time to upgrade from AM4 anymore. If you make the jump today, you'll pay way more for RAM than you probably ever thought you would. On the other hand, waiting another two or three years, hoping prices come back down, means being too late to AM5 and missing out on drop-in CPU upgrades. And let's not forget there's no guarantee you won't be paying the same prices, or even more, by then. I'd rather bite the bullet and pay the RAM premium than spend years gambling that prices will go in my favor.
The 5800X3D is still legendary, but the platform is well past its prime.

Well first of all I am not a PC gamer, so I don't need the ""latest and greatest"".  In fact, until this year, I was using a Core 2 Quad desktop and a Core 2 Duo laptop.  Both were running Windows 7, and were upgraded to 8 GB of RAM.

With the motherboard in my desktop failing, and Windows 7 being more than five years out of security updates, I decided it was time to upgrade.

Due to the outlandish cost of new computers, and modern RAM modules, I decided to buy secondhand computers from the DDR3 generation.  For a good used desktop and a good used laptop, plus 16 GB of used DDR3 RAM for each, I paid less than $300. 73,2026-10-05,"Analysts say Flavio Bolsonaro is projected to win a run-off vote on October 25 and become Brazil’s next president.
Save
Share
Right-wing Brazilian Senator Flavio Bolsonaro has exceeded expectations in the first-round vote with a slight lead over the incumbent left-wing President Luiz Inacio ‌Lula da Silva, triggering a run-off later this month.
“Brazil wants change, and I am very happy with the results,” Bolsonaro told reporters in Brasilia on Sunday as he saluted “the end of the era of [Lula’s] Workers’ Party”.
The 45-year-old ⁠son of right-wing former President Jair Bolsonaro, who is serving a 27-year jail sentence for plotting a coup after losing the 2022 election, now faces an October 25 showdown with the 80-year-old Lula, who is seeking a fourth nonconsecutive term.
‌In the run-up to Sunday’s first-round vote, a series of polls showed a close race between Lula and Bolsonaro, with the incumbent president poised to win by about 3 percentage points against his right-wing challenger.
In a surprise result that defied opinion poll projections, Bolsonaro beat Lula with 47 percent of the vote, while Lula got 45.1 percent.
Neither reached the 50 percent threshold needed to win in the first round. That sets up another Lula-Bolsonaro showdown, four years after Lula narrowly defeated Jair Bolsonaro.
Bolsonaro’s stronger-than-expected performance is likely to buoy his campaign and may bolster Brazil’s currency and stock market, which have risen on the prospects of the business-friendly challenger.
Oliver Stuenkel, professor of international relations at the Getulio Vargas Foundation (FGV) in San Paulo, Brazil, said Lula was in a difficult position as he could not count on voters who backed one of the other 10 candidates to vote for him in the second round.
“Not only is he behind now after this first round, but the other candidates who received a total of nearly 8 percent are largely centre-right, which means that the people who voted for these other candidates are very unlikely to shift for Lula,” Stuenkel told Al Jazeera.
“So he must change his campaign tactics and above all mobilise a significant part of people who stayed at home,” he added. About 30 million Brazilians – 21 percent of the electorate – did not vote in the first round.
Al Jazeera’s Monica Yanakiew, reporting from Rio de Janeiro, said Lula supporters felt their candidate had not used social media as effectively as the right-wing camp. “This is something they have to work on in the second round,” Yanakiew said.
From a historical perspective, however, such efforts are unlikely to change the outcome. “Unless something very unusual happens in the next three weeks, Flavio Bolsonaro is set to win,” Stuenkel said. “In Brazilian presidential politics, no candidate who came second in the first round ended up winning the presidency since 1989.”
Several politicians from the Bolsonaro family have won congressional races taking place as part of the broader nationwide general elections.
Michelle Bolsonaro, wife of former President Jair Bolsonaro and stepmother of Flavio, won a Senate seat in the Federal District, home to the capital, Brasilia.
In June, the 44-year-old, who has never held government office, had a public falling out with her stepson, whom she accused of disrespecting her in lengthy videos posted on her social media. The pair later made up.
Ex-leader Bolsonaro’s second son Carlos Bolsonaro, 43, was the second most-voted senatorial candidate in the southern Santa Catarina state and won a seat in the Senate.
The fourth and younger son, Jair Renan Bolsonaro, was elected to the lower house of Congress.
Some experts credit “antipetismo” — a trend of hostility against Lula’s PT movement — with fuelling the Bolsonaro family’s rise to political power.
Bolsonaro’s Liberal Party (PL) won 121 of the 513 seats in the Chamber of Deputies while Lula’s PT got 70.
Brazil’s presidential election carries significant regional and geopolitical implications.
Reporting from Sao Paulo, Al Jazeera’s Lucia Newman said Lula has been “on a collision course” with United States President Donald Trump, who is a vocal and ardent supporter of the Bolsonaro camp.
“That puts Brazil in the middle of a fierce competition between Washington and China for political, diplomatic and economic dominance, especially since Brazil is a powerhouse on all these fronts,” Newman said.
While Bolsonarism is strongly aligned with the US and Israel, Brazil also has a tight economic relationship with China, its largest trade partner. Bolsonaro has portrayed Trump as an ally and is expected to strengthen ties with Washington if elected.
In case of a win, the younger Bolsonaro has pledged to seek a congressional amnesty for his father, who is in prison over his role in a coup plot following his election loss to Lula by 1.8 percentage points in 2022. That would need approval from both houses of Congress, which are currently dominated by conservatives.
Similar to Trump, the Bolsonaros have made rigged election claims. The insurrection that shook Brasilia in January 2023 shared similarities with the storming of the US Capitol two years prior. Like Trump, Bolsonaro has also pledged to release his father’s supporters responsible for the coup.
Concerns about US intervention in Brazil’s domestic affairs have also loomed over the campaign. Lula has accused the Trump administration of using tariffs and other means to interfere with Brazilian affairs.
Vinicius de Carvalho, a lecturer in Brazilian studies at King’s College London, UK, stressed that Latin America has been experiencing a right-wing wave, with the election of US-aligned governments. This includes Ecuador, El Salvador, Panama and Paraguay.
By contrast, Lula was part of the so-called “pink tide” in Latin America, a wave of left-leaning governments that came to power in the late 1990s and early 2000s. With Congress stacked against him, if Lula is re-elected, he will still need to “respond to some demands of the right”, de Carvalho said.

75,2026-10-05,"Most Widely Read Newspaper
Nigerian Stock Market trade floor (Photo Credit: Bloomberg)
Kindly share this story:
A wave of profit-taking dragged the Nigerian equities market lower during a holiday-shortened trading week, as investors realigned portfolios following recent monetary policy shifts. The resulting drop in trading turnover highlighted a cautious market stance, even as institutional activity sustained heavy trading in financial stocks and sovereign debt instruments, writes JIDE AJIA
 The Nigerian equities market closed the four-day trading week in negative territory as profit-taking across major sectors dragged main indices lower, while overall trading activity contracted significantly following the public holiday declared on Thursday, 1 October, 2026, in commemoration of Independence Day celebrations.
Market sentiment reflected a broader macroeconomic adjustment following the Central Bank of Nigeria’s major monetary policy reset, where the benchmark interest rate was cut by 350 basis points to 23 per cent alongside cooling headline inflation at 15.39 per cent.
This shift in the interest rate environment has prompted portfolio realignments across fixed income and equity instruments, driving mixed trading behaviours. Overall market breadth on the Exchange closed negative, with 37 equities depreciating against 44 gainers and 65 stocks remaining unchanged. 
Index, sector performanceSee more Punch stories on Google.Add Punch on Google
The NGX All-Share Index and Market Capitalisation depreciated by 0.52 per cent and 0.50 per cent to close the week at 250,808.27 and N162.843tn respectively, down from 252,113.41 points and N163.689tn recorded in the previous week.
Sectoral performance was predominantly bearish, led by declines in the NGX CG Index, which fell 1.43 per cent, the NGX Premium Index down 1.38 per cent, the NGX Banking Index dropping 1.33 per cent, and the NGX Consumer Goods Index falling 0.91 per cent.        
The NGX 30 Index also dipped 0.52 per cent to settle at 9,230.30 points. Bucking the market downturn, modest gains were registered in the NGX Growth Index, which rose 1.85 per cent, the NGX Insurance Index gaining 0.61 per cent, the NGX Main Board Index up 0.08 per cent, and the NGX Oil & Gas Index creeping up 0.05 per cent, while the NGX Commodity Index remained flat.
Volumes, sector performance  Related News  NPF Pensions eyes Dangote refinery investments  Nigeria’s 73% financial inclusion leaves 60.4m vulnerable  NGX edges lower as insurance gains soften market losses   
Investors traded a total turnover of 3.166 billion shares worth N155.023bn in 206,662 deals, representing a sharp reduction compared to the 4.689 billion shares valued at N240.824bn that exchanged hands in 261,198 deals the preceding week.
The Financial Services Industry maintained its dominant lead by volume, accounting for 1.944 billion shares valued at N77.733 billion in 92,035 deals, thereby contributing 61.41 per cent to total equity turnover volume and 50.14 per cent to total value.
The Investment Industry followed in second place with 409.484 million shares worth N5.400bn in 1,444 deals, while the ICT Industry placed third with a turnover of 231.705 million shares worth N9.606bn in 24,133 deals.
The top three equities measured by volume were Fidelity Bank Plc, VFD Group Plc, and Access Holdings Plc, which collectively accounted for 1.343 billion shares worth N27.580bn in 15,771 deals, contributing 42.42 per cent to total volume and 17.79 per cent to total equity turnover value.
Securities, listings 
   
Exchange-Traded Products saw 1.684 million units valued at N379.449m traded in 4,327 deals, compared to 2.660 million units valued at N747.735m in 6,048 deals the previous week. In the bond segment, 81,063 units worth N85.606m were traded in 48 deals compared to 180,459 units valued at N179.624m in 43 deals prior.
The primary market recorded significant corporate capital-raising activity, including the listing of 3.206 billion rights issue shares for Guinea Insurance Plc, 3.684 billion private placement shares for Regency Alliance Insurance Plc, and 1.110 billion combined rights issue and public offer shares for The Initiates Plc.
Additionally, new and supplementary Federal Government of Nigeria bonds issued in September 2026 were formally admitted to the Exchange’s Daily Official List, led by the 10-year 16.79 per cent FGN SEP 2036 paper valued at N888.633bn.

In the bond segment, 81,063 units worth N85.606m were traded in 48 deals compared to 180,459 units valued at N179.624m in 43 deals prior.
The primary market recorded significant corporate capital-raising activity, including the listing of 3.206 billion rights issue shares for Guinea Insurance Plc, 3.684 billion private placement shares for Regency Alliance Insurance Plc, and 1.110 billion combined rights issue and public offer shares for The Initiates Plc.
Additionally, new and supplementary Federal Government of Nigeria bonds issued in September 2026 were formally admitted to the Exchange’s Daily Official List, led by the 10-year 16.79 per cent FGN SEP 2036 paper valued at N888.633bn.
                    
The Investment Industry followed in second place with 409.484 million shares worth N5.400bn in 1,444 deals, while the ICT Industry placed third with a turnover of 231.705 million shares worth N9.606bn in 24,133 deals.
The top three equities measured by volume were Fidelity Bank Plc, VFD Group Plc, and Access Holdings Plc, which collectively accounted for 1.343 billion shares worth N27.580bn in 15,771 deals, contributing 42.42 per cent to total volume and 17.79 per cent to total equity turnover value.

76,2026-10-05,"Pay Dirt is Slate’s money advice column. Have a question? Send it to Kristin and Ilyce here. (It’s anonymous!)
Dear Pay Dirt,
My stepmother passed away recently. One of her friends had power of attorney and was taking her to appointments, for which my stepmom was paying her a small monthly stipend. After my stepmom’s passing, the friend discovered she was co-owner of an account with a dollar amount in the low six figures. My brother, my stepmom’s biological son and executor of the estate, was left everything according to the will. The friend was pressured to hand over the co-owned account, which she did. Now she is having second thoughts. I know you can’t offer specific legal advice, but might it be possible she would have standing to sue to get it back?  I plan to stay out of the drama should things progress any further.
—Stepmother Drama

Dear Stepmother Drama,
I wonder why your stepmom listed her friend as co-owner on the account. Was it to help her pay bills or because she wanted her friend to have that cash?
If the account had rights of survivorship, which many joint accounts do by default, the funds would have passed directly to the friend the moment your stepmother died, regardless of what the will said, the same way a house held in joint tenancy automatically passes to the surviving owner. If that’s the case, the money was legally hers, and your brother had no claim.
But if the account was added purely for convenience, so she could help pay bills or get access for care logistics, without the intent of leaving her the funds, that’s a murkier situation. And in that case, a court might side with the will’s actual intent.
Either way, if she felt pressured to hand the money over before she understood what was going on or what her rights were as co-owner of the account, it’s not too late to ask. She should dig up the paperwork she signed when the account was opened and bring it to an estate attorney for a consultation.
That’s the legal side. Ethically, if she knew her friend wanted everything to go to her biological son, she needs to set aside the idea there’s a six-figure amount of cash with her name on it and think about who her friend would have wanted to get that sum.
As an aside, staying out of the drama is exactly what you should do, no matter what happens between the friend and your brother.

      Please keep questions short (<150 words), and don’t submit the same question to multiple columns. We are unable to edit or remove questions after publication. Use pseudonyms to maintain anonymity. Your submission may be used in other Slate advice columns and may be edited for publication.
    
Thanks! Your question has been submitted.
Dear Pay Dirt, 
I’m wondering how my partner and I (unmarried) can think of finances during a stint living in another city. My partner and I are planning to move to another expensive city for one-to-two years. I’m happy to go, but this move is really for my partner, who wants to be in a new environment for a couple of years. For me, it’s not ideal timing, but I do love the city we’re going to and will enjoy it. We both want to settle in our home city and have parents with not urgent but slowly increasing needs, so it will definitely only be a year or two.
My partner earns much more than me (twice what I earn now and will likely be earning more in a new job when we move), and comes from a wealthy family. I’m freelance and working on a Ph.D. I earn enough for myself, but I don’t have a pension or sick pay. I’m very lucky to own a house, but I have no other family or financial support or safety net. Some of my work can be done remotely when we move, and I can easily travel back and forth once or twice per term for my Ph.D. if needed, but I’ll be giving up some regular paid teaching work so my income will be going down when we move.
The other complication is that we both own houses. He has a big house with several bedrooms he could rent out. He also has a mortgage. I own a very small house outright, no mortgage, but legally I could only rent out the small spare room, not the whole residence. I could do this, but the limited finances versus the possibility of damage to the house is giving me pause. When we move, we would be renting an apartment, so technically my expenses would be going up because I don’t currently have to pay rent or a mortgage. I’m trying to think about what would be fair for us financially. It would make sense for us to keep my house as a base and have him rent out his house, but it makes me a bit uncomfortable that he would be making money and building capital (or benefiting by paying off his mortgage) during the move from that rent while I wouldn’t be making money, and would in fact be losing money by suddenly paying rent. I also don’t know how we should be accounting for his salary increasing during this move while my income will go down, and how we should account for this in thinking about expenses and rent.
My partner is very open to these discussions. He knows that I’m more financially vulnerable than he is, but neither of us has had to think about shared finances in this way, so we would appreciate some guidance or ways of thinking about it.
—New City, New Complications
Dear New City, New Complications,
There are a number of ways partners (married and unmarried) go about sharing expenses. The easiest way is to compare your income to your partner’s, and then divide all expenses by that percentage. For example, if he earns $100,000 annually and you earn $50,000, he would pay two-thirds of all shared expenses. (Because he is making two-thirds of of your total combined income, and you are making one-third of the total.)
On the other hand, you own assets separately, like your two homes. He has expenses for this (his mortgage, taxes, and insurance) while you only have to pay taxes and insurance. He could rent out his property, while you legally cannot rent out all of yours.
You’ve said it makes sense to keep your home as your base rather than renting it out. If that’s the plan, then your partner should pay you two-thirds of its current market rent, the same ratio you’re using for everything else. Why? You’re the one giving up income by keeping a house that’s available to you both. You’ve got expenses for that property that need to be paid, and those should be counted in the total expenses involved for the move. He’s benefiting from having a paid-off home to return to, rent-free, while his own property is earning money. That asymmetry is feeding into your unease about the move overall. It’s making a financially unequal situation (the move benefits him financially more than you) even more lopsided.
If you’re still feeling overwhelmed, one way to make this calculation easier and cleaner is for you to sell your home. If you’ve lived there for two out of the last five years, you could keep your profits tax free. That money would be yours alone, and you could then invest it elsewhere. When you visit your home town, you could stay with friends or rent an Airbnb or stay in a hotel. This would give you the opportunity to experience different parts of the city and perhaps help you decide where to live when you move back.
In the meantime, it’s important to start the money conversation now, before you’ve committed to any expenses in your new town. You and your partner should recognize what you’re giving up (extra income) and taking on (additional expenses) before committing to any new expenses in your new city.
—Ilyce
My brother “Tom’s” wife, “Laura,” cheated on him and left him for a mutual friend. He’s been a wreck for the last four months. Last week, my husband, “Al,” who’s close with Tom, came to me to say that Tom had been discussing increasingly violent fantasies about hurting Laura in a group chat they’re both in with other male relatives.

Are you ready to take the business world by storm, Capricorn? Your innate work ethic and unwavering discipline are your superpowers. You’re great at knowing your strengths and using them to set achievable goals. You’re not afraid to dream big, but you’re also realistic: the perfect combination for success. Whether you’re looking to launch your own side hustle or head straight into running your own business, a self-run startup is a great fit for you. Your approach is less about reinventing the wheel and more about making it the most reliable one on the market. 
Virgo, your logic skills make you particularly suited to entrepreneurship. Mercury rules your sign, giving you the power to communicate your innovative ideas with precision and charm. As you toy with the idea of launching your own business, think of areas where your analytical prowess can shine. 
Choose something that you’re passionate about and that matches your skillset – that should almost be easy for you, Virgo. Your high standards for yourself and your work will set you apart from others in the same market. 
Leo, your natural leadership ability is magnetic, drawing in people who share your vision. It would be easy for you to attract both clients and other like-minded employees. Your charisma will bring others to your business, but your generosity and talent will keep them there. 
You’re someone who doesn’t mind thinking about the long term, so as long as you keep an eye on the future and continue building yourself along with your business, you’ll be a big success. Plus, your eye for luxury and aesthetics means any venture you choose to run would be top quality.

Bank of Japan Deputy Governor Shinichi Uchida attends a press conference after a BOJ policy meeting in Tokyo, Japan, June 16, 2026. REUTERS/Kim Kyung-Hoon
TOKYO, Oct 5 : Bank of Japan Deputy Governor Shinichi Uchida said the global AI boom may have eased financial conditions by stoking demand and boosting asset prices, but warned of the risk of a market pullback if expected profits fail to materialize.
""It is a big positive demand shock, which has put upward pressure on the economy and prices,"" Uchida said on worldwide AI adoption in the text of a speech on the central bank's website on Monday.
The technology could also raise productivity and enhance capital stock accumulation, which in turn might affect a country's natural rate of interest, he said.
""Tentatively, it appears the demand side has come first and made financial conditions more accommodative on balance,"" Uchida said.

        A version of this recipe first appeared in The Bite, Salon's food newsletter. Sign up for early access to articles like this, plus recipes, food-related pop culture recommendations and conversations about what we're eating, how and why.    
There comes a point in the life of many soups when they get a little sleepy.
This happens especially often with soups that are creamy, starchy or â blessedly â both. Everything is humming along just fine. The onions are soft. The garlic is fragrant. The spices have bloomed. You taste a spoonful and think, Hmm. So you add another pinch of seasoning. You taste again. Still: Hmm.
At this point, most of us instinctively reach for the salt.
But Iâd like to gently intercept your hand on the way there and redirect it toward wherever you keep your vinegar. Some swear by the pantry, others insist upon the refrigerator; we will not allow ourselves to be distracted from our soup scholarship by these sectarian squabbles (at least not this week).
Because sometimes your soup doesnât need more salt. It needs acid.
Related
A small splash of vinegar at the end of cooking can give a tired pot an almost startling jolt. Vegetables taste brighter. Herbs seem greener. Starchy soups feel less pasty on the tongue. Spices that had faded into the background suddenly become legible again. Depending on how much you add, you might even get a delectable little Oh, what is that? pucker at the end of each bite.
I love lemon in soup, too. Sometimes I want the lemon flavor to be the whole point: a lemony avgolemono, for instance, or a chicken stew that tastes unabashedly citrusy. But lemon can announce itself loudly. Same with lime.
Vinegar can be more like a gentle wake-up than a glass of water to the face.
I first really understood this while making Alison Romanâs dill-packed potato-leek soup, published originally a little over five years ago, which finishes with white wine vinegar. Roman writes that the vinegar makes the soup taste âalmost like a pickle.â I went into the recipe expecting exactly that, was delighted to find it delivered and started experimenting with the same trick in soups of my own.
The key here is finishing with vinegar. There are, of course, soups where acidity is built in earlier: Chinese hot-and-sour soups, borscht and various Eastern European sour soups all have their own traditions and techniques. But thatâs a different lesson.
For our purposes, cook your soup until itâs essentially done. Then, before making the final salt adjustment, add about 1/2 teaspoon to 1 teaspoon of vinegar. Stir it in thoroughly and give it a moment. I actually like to step away from the stove at this point. Get some water. Wash a spoon. Pop my head out the kitchen window like a dog on a road trip. Then come back and taste again.
Vinegar is firmly in the category of ingredients that are easier to add than subtract. Youâre not necessarily trying to make the soup taste vinegary. Youâre waiting for everything else in the pot to suddenly taste more like itself.
Want more great food writing and recipes? Sign up for Salonâs free food newsletter, The Bite.
Different vinegars naturally play better with different soups. Red wine vinegar is wonderful with hearty lentil, bean and farro soups, and I love it in beef stew. It can also rescue a tomato soup thatâs drifted too far toward sweetness.
White wine vinegar â or even plain distilled white vinegar â is excellent with potatoes, creamy chicken soups and corn chowders. Think of the logic of salt-and-vinegar potato chips: starch loves that sharp little counterpoint.
For sweeter, earthier soups made with squash or root vegetables, I tend toward vinegars with a little fruitiness of their own, like apple cider vinegar or my current favorite, white balsamic.
There are exceptions. I probably wouldnât start splashing vinegar into a delicate seafood bisque. And broccoli-cheddar soup, for me, remains a no-fly zone. Not every pot requires intervention.
But many do.
I learned to cook thinking that âfinishingâ a dish meant adding enough salt and pepper. Then fresh herbs were a revelation. Acid was another one: a reminder that a finished dish doesnât necessarily need more flavor so much as it sometimes needs contrast.
We need your help to stay independent.
Thatâs especially true of this weekâs soup: a hot honey carrot and sweet potato number built with garlic, onion, coriander, paprika, cumin, red pepper flakes and miso, then rounded out with savory stock and coconut milk.
Before the vinegar, it tasted good â but a little too sweet and a little too earthy. The white balsamic changed the geometry of the whole bowl. The sweetness of the vegetables became more nuanced instead of landing like a sledgehammer. The spices emerged. The stock tasted more savory. And instead of registering immediately, the hot honey left a gentle warmth at the back of each bite.
So thatâs your homework for week two of Soup School: When your next pot tastes fine but somehow unfinished, donât automatically add another shower of salt.
Add a little vinegar.
Let it sink in.
Then taste again.
Building on last weekâs lesson, this soup starts in the oven, where carrots, sweet potatoes, scallions, miso, hot honey and a deliberately assertive amount of spice get deeply caramelized before ever meeting the stockpot. Coconut milk softens the edges.Â 
Then, incorporating this weekâs lesson, white balsamic or apple cider vinegar keeps the root vegetablesâ sweetness from becoming cloying.
A note on the spices: There are a lot of them. Thatâs intentional. Sweet potatoes and carrots can take it, and the coconut milk mellows everything considerably. If youâre particularly sensitive to heat, though, feel free to scale back the red pepper flakes and hot honey.
Ingredients
2 medium carrots, roughly chopped
1 cup diced sweet potato, fresh or frozen
2 tablespoons sliced scallion greens, plus more for serving
1 tablespoon olive oil
3 teaspoons garlic powder
3 teaspoons onion powder
3 teaspoons ground coriander
3 teaspoons smoked paprika
3 teaspoons ground cumin
3 teaspoons red pepper flakes, or less to taste
1 tablespoon white miso paste
3 teaspoons hot honey, plus more to taste if desired
1 cup chicken or vegetable stock, divided
1 cup full-fat canned coconut milk
White balsamic vinegar or apple cider vinegar, as needed
Kosher salt, to taste
Coconut cream, for serving
Crusty bread, for serving
Â 
Directions

82,2026-10-05,"Rumored Home Security Camera From Apple Won’t Actually Record Video
Search

Apple, a company known for making privacy a priority, is reportedly working on a home security camera that doesn’t record video or photos. 
The report comes from trusted Apple insider Mark Gurman who was speaking on his brand new podcast Power On, an extension of his newsletter published on Bloomberg. Gurman says that the camera will sit within a “new Apple smart home ecosystem.”
“They’re also building home security products, both first-party and third-party home security products. And the biggest thing is a program called J450. And this is an offshoot and a companion product to the J490 smart home display. And these are little cameras that you can put around your house. They have a very low frame rate. It looks like a cylinder. It looks like a metal giant, you know, chapstick,” Gurman says per The Verge.
“And what does that mean? No video recording. So it’s a sensor that cannot actually pull in real video footage. It’s analyzing your environment in your home and around your home simply using AI.” 
Gurman says that this strange new camera technology is “very similar” to the one Apple is reportedly developing for its camera-equipped AirPods. 
The home camera that Gurman describes will have facial recognition technology and will be able to tell the user things like “Mark has just walked into the room,” et cetera. But it will not be able to pull a video recording of that person walking into a room. Gurman says this system is in response to a society that is increasingly monitored by systems like Flock cameras. 
“They have pitched this as an alternative to that very disturbing idea of mass surveillance because you’re not getting anything but text descriptions,” Gurman says. “There’s nothing you can do with it, there’s no video recording. This is going to be the first home video security camera that doesn’t give you any footage. I think that’s a pretty compelling pitch.” 
Gurman points out that there are instances where users will want footage from a home security camera, like a burglary. 
“What are you supposed to do with the [text] description? Give it to the police? They’re not going to be able to do anything with the description; they want the actual tape. So it will be interesting to see how they market this thing,” he adds.

Leaked reports from tech outlet YTECHB indicate that the international market will receive the standard Find X10 and a top-tier Find X10 Pro Max, replacing the traditional Pro variant.
Oppo Find X10 Pro Max
The standard OPPO Find X10 will reportedly arrive in Europe starting at €1,399 (~$1,567) for the 12GB/256GB model, while the 12GB/512GB variant is slated for €1,599 (~$1,791). This marks a steep €400 (~$448) price surge compared to the previous Find X9 generation. Meanwhile, the flagship Find X10 Pro Max is expected to retail at €1,799 (~$2,015) for the 12GB/512GB configuration, substantially higher than the €1,299 (~$1,455) introductory price of its predecessor, the Find X9 Pro. Industry analysts attribute these price hikes to escalating component expenses, particularly rising production costs for high-density flash memory and mobile RAM.
Hardware specifications for the global variants align closely with the Chinese models. The base Find X10 features a 6.59-inch 1.5K LTPO AMOLED display running at 144Hz with a peak brightness of 2,000 nits. It is powered by the MediaTek Dimensity 9600M platform, paired with LPDDR5X RAM and fast UFS 4.1 storage. Its camera module includes a 200 MP primary camera with optical image stabilization, supported by an 8,000 mAh battery with 80W wired SuperVOOC and 50W wireless charging.
The premium Find X10 Pro Max upgrades performance with the flagship MediaTek Dimensity 9600 Pro chipset and a larger 6.78-inch LTPO AMOLED screen. Co-engineered with Hasselblad, its rear imaging system integrates a triple 200 MP camera setup, including a Samsung ISOCELL periscope telephoto sensor offering 3x optical zoom. Both models run Android 17 with ColorOS 17, carry comprehensive IP68/IP69 water resistance, and support Wi-Fi 7 and Bluetooth 6.2. With launch countdowns active on regional portals, an official international rollout appears imminent.
